8 Masterpiece Detective Shows That Are Better Than 'Sherlock'

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Most people around the globe agree that Sherlock Holmes is the greatest fictional detective of all time, with a legacy that has endured for over a century across literature, cinema, games, and countless other forms of art and entertainment. Among the many film and television projects made about Sherlock Holmes over the years, the BBC’s Sherlock is unarguably one of the most beloved. A modern reimagining of Arthur Conan Doyle’s darling detective, the show remains a television landmark of the 21st century. However, the world of detective fiction is exhaustive and forever expanding, with endless new stories that deserve your attention, including some that arguably overshadow this popular icon.

These shows are not necessarily bigger in scale, but they bring a new narrative style to the genre, explore conventions from a new perspective, or feature more complex, mind-bending mysteries that can leave fans in shock, awe, and, in some cases, inspired. With every detective an intriguing character, each of these shows is a must-watch for genre fans. So, without further ado, here’s our selection of detective shows that make Sherlock look small.

A psychological crime thriller written and created by Neil Cross, Luther revolves around the titular detective, John Luther, who is famed for taking down notorious criminals but also infamous for getting consumed by his cases, often resulting in violent and obsessive behavior. The series follows his relentless mission to thwart crime while navigating his personal demons, but mostly his arch-nemesis, Alice Morgan. Idris Elba stars as Luther and Ruth Wilson as Alice, with Steven Mackintosh, Saskia Reeves, Warren Brown, and Dermot Crowley in other notable roles.

A highly acclaimed and popular police procedural of its time, Luther brings a darker, graver tone to the genre, with more emphasis on the psychological narrative of its characters. Idris Elba is widely acclaimed for his powerhouse performance as the troubled detective, as is Ruth Wilson for her impeccable turn as Alice. During its original five-season run, Luther earned sweeping accolades and was widely acclaimed for reviving the form of hard-boiled detective noir with morally grey themes and a contemporary setting.

Created by Joe Penhall, Netflix’s Mindhunter is based on the non-fiction book Mindhunter: Inside the FBI's Elite Serial Crime Unit by FBI agent John E. Douglas and Mark Olshaker. Dramatizing the founding of the agency’s Behavioral Science Unit in the 1970s, the series follows agents Holden Ford and Bill Tench, as well as psychologist Wendy Carr, as they interview imprisoned serial killers and profile them, hoping to use the data to solve ongoing cases. Jonathan Groff, Anna Torv, Holt McCallany, Cotter Smith, and Hannah Gross star in key roles.

Mindhunter puts a deep analytical spin on detective fiction with strong elements of the psychological horror, crime thriller, and murder mystery genres. Through fictional interviews with some of the most notorious killers in American history, like Charles Manson, Jerry Brudos, and Son of Sam, it provides chilling psychological background to their infamous crimes. With its near-perfect storytelling inspired by major real-life crimes and compelling performances that leave a lasting impression, Mindhunter has been widely praised as one of the best true crime shows on Netflix.

Created by Robert Doherty, Elementary is a contemporary reimagination of Arthur Conan Doyle’s Sherlock Holmes, but with very different stories and characterizations, transporting the British detective from London to New York City as a recovering addict. Starring Jonny Lee Miller as Sherlock Holmes and Lucy Liu as Dr. Watson, the CBS drama follows the duo as they help the NYPD with complex cases, using Sherlock’s genius deduction methods and Watson’s investigative skills. Aidan Quinn, Natalie Dormer, Rhys Ifans, Jon Michael Hill, and John Noble round out the main cast.

Elementary never feels like a pastiche of a literary icon; rather, it simply takes the classic characters and themes and invents new stories, modern crimes, and character backstories in a high-energy, modern procedural. Even the case-of-the-week stories are not adapted from Doyle’s originals, making this ABC hit series intriguingly original. Sherlock fans are sure to appreciate the reinvented Watson, Mycroft, and Moriarty, but most importantly Lee Miller’s Sherlock, which is considered one of the best portrayals of Holmes on television.

A great detective is really just a personality with a method attached, which is why we keep remaking the same four of them. Here is yours, what it says about you, and the case for them being the best in the business.

You let people think you are not a threat and then you simply never go away. Columbo is the only major detective show that tells you who did it in the first ten minutes, because the pleasure was never the puzzle, it was watching a rich man slowly realise he is in trouble.

You are the most interesting person in the room and you know exactly how to use it. Rian Johnson built Blanc as a gentleman detective who is also an outsider everywhere he goes, which lets him be charming at people who would never otherwise talk to him.

Everybody tells you everything, and you have never once asked them to. Jessica Fletcher is a crime novelist from a small town in Maine who is so warm and so obviously trustworthy that suspects confess to her essentially as a social courtesy.

You expect to be lied to and you are almost never disappointed. Chandler wrote Marlowe as a man with a rigid private code in a city that has none, which is why he takes the case, solves it, gets nothing out of it and takes the next one.

An anthology crime thriller series created by Nic Pizzolatto, True Detective explores various stories of uncanny crimes and the local police’s attempts to seek truth and deliver justice, while also navigating their personal demons. Each season follows a new homicide case revolving around a disparate set of characters, with the settings spanning Alaska, Arkansas, California, and Louisiana. The show’s revolving ensemble cast features Matthew McConaughey, Woody Harrelson, Colin Farrell, Rachel McAdams, Jodie Foster, and more across seasons.

True Detective is diverse in its storytelling, with styles spanning neo-noir to Southern Gothic, true-crime, and Nordic noir, making every season feel like an expansive new show. But all seasons are bound by the underlying philosophical themes, unsettling local legends, and deeply flawed detectives, which makes True Detective way darker, more complex, and layered than Sherlock. Since its premiere over a decade ago, True Detective has remained highly respected and has won major accolades, including several Emmys and a BAFTA.

Rian Johnson’s first major TV show as a creator, Poker Face is a crime comedy series that uses a case-of-the-week format, centering on Charlie Cale, a casino employee in Nevada who happens to be a bona fide human lie detector. After getting on the wrong side of her mobster boss, Charlie goes on the run, traveling across the country and solving murders using her unique ability and remarkable social skills. Natasha Lyonne stars as Charlie, with Benjamin Bratt, Ron Perlman, Rhea Perlman, Simon Helberg, Patti Harrison, and Steve Buscemi in recurring roles, and several famous guest stars appearing in individual episodes, including Giancarlo Esposito, Katie Holmes, and more.

Unlike most contemporary detective shows or case-of-the-week procedurals, Poker Face is a “howcatchem” that takes inspiration from classic detective series like Columbo and Magnum, P.I. Johnson brings his signature brand of mystery storytelling to this colorful ride with a quirky nomad who entirely changes the perspective of the genre. Driven largely by Natasha Lyonne’s live-wire performance, often compared to Peter Falk’s, Poker Face is a fantastic detective show with mature themes beneath its fun exterior.

A British sci-fi mystery thriller created by Paul Tomalin, Bodies is a Netflix adaptation of Si Spencer’s DC Vertigo comic book series. The show follows a murder mystery that spans generations, time, and space. The plot follows four different detectives who try to solve the same mystery of a dead body found in a London back alley in four different timelines. The series stars Stephen Graham, Amaka Okafor, Kyle Soller, Jacob Fortune-Lloyd, Shira Haas, and Greta Scacchi in key roles.

A unique, genre-blending, and mind-bending sci-fi show, Bodies is detective noir, high-concept sci-fi, and period drama, all rolled into one. Highly suspenseful, with unexpected outcomes in every episode, Bodies keeps the audience constantly on the edge, confounded by the story’s movement and engrossed in the convincing performances. Though the show quickly went off the radar after its release, the Netflix original has been critically praised for its elevated narrative and incredible cinematography, and is a must-watch for mystery fans.

A multi-Emmy Award-winning mystery comedy drama series created by Andy Breckman, Monk follows the pursuits of former San Francisco police detective Adrian Monk, who suffers from obsessive-compulsive disorder and various phobias, which worsen after his wife’s mysterious death. Gifted (and cursed) with sharp observation skills and odd crime-solving methods, Monk solves various crimes as a consultant for the SFPD while navigating his afflictions, which often become exhausting for everyone around him, especially his assistant. Tony Shalhoub, Ted Levine, Bitty Schram, Jason Gray-Stanford, and Traylor Howard star in the main roles.

Powered by Shalhoub’s phenomenal performance as the so-called “defective detective,” Monk is an inverted Sherlock. Adrian Monk is everything Holmes is, and not at the same time, which makes this detective series one of a kind. Charming and heartwarming, with its odd-ball detective, ample humor, and character-driven stories, the comedy procedural is exceptionally addictive. Often hailed as one of the best crime shows of the 21st century, Monk puts a more accessible, comedic twist on the traditional detective persona.

Created by Clive Exton and Brian Eastman, Agatha Christie’s Poirot is a serialized TV adaptation of Agatha Christie’s popular crime fiction series featuring the titular detective, Hercule Poirot. Starring David Suchet as the famous Belgian detective, the series follows his meticulous investigations as he travels around the world solving the most complicated crimes, with his friend, Captain Arthur Hastings, and Inspector James Japp of Scotland Yard by his side. Hugh Fraser, Phillip Jackson, Pauline Moran, and Zoë Wanamaker play recurring roles across the show’s 13 seasons, alongside a rotating ensemble guest cast.

A British television landmark, the ITV show successfully and faithfully adapts almost every Poirot novel and short story penned by Christie. Suchet brings life to the persnickety detective with his near-perfect depiction, which is often considered the definitive screen version of the mystery icon. Spanning 13 seasons and 25 years, Agatha Christie’s Poirot is an exhaustive detective show that does every bit of justice to the setting, storytelling, and character design of the Poirot universe as created by Christie.

Would You Rather: Cadillac Escalade IQ vs. Escalade-V

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

For about $160,000, are you taking the 682-hp supercharged gas Escalade or the 750-hp electric one?

A lot of companies build EV models that have an internal-combustion analogue, with the guiding principle of, “the car you liked before, but electric.” There’s a tight thematic cohesiveness to that approach—a BMW i3 and M340i each endeavor to arrive at their optimized level of BMW 3-ness despite the differing powertrains—but the Cadillac Escalade is not like that. Yes, there are gas and electric versions, but they’re similar only inasmuch as they each avidly pursue the high-level Escalade goals of cosseting bigness and cocky braggadocio. When Tony Soprano got an Escalade in season 5 of The Sopranos, the only question around that casting decision was, what took so long? Today Tony would have more choices in his pursuit of the maximum expression of GM SUV, as do any of us with about $160,000 to spend. Namely, would you rather have a supercharged V-8 Escalade-V or an electric Escalade IQ?

Cadillac designed the Escalade-V’s exhaust to be extra boisterous, adding a 360-degree loop to the passenger’s-side pipe to equalize the length with the driver’s-side counterpart, for maximum sonic awesomeness. The IQ does not have equal-length dual exhaust. Whatever exhaust it does produce will get you into a tedious and possibly confrontational discussion of well-to-wheel emissions with your uncle at Thanksgiving. However, you’ll never have to worry about thieves stealing your catalytic converters, so I’ll call that one a draw.

See all results for used cars for sale near 97754

Looking at the specs, I’m surprised to see that the Escalade-V weighs eight pounds more than the IQ. Hold on, that doesn’t sound right—let me check my notes—sorry, my curb-weight figure for the Escalade-V inadvertently included a Toyota GR86 parked on its roof. Remove that extra car, and the supercharged Escalade is quite a bit lighter: 6290 pounds for the V, and a pavement-crushing 9120 pounds for the IQ.

Despite the weight disparity, the two Slades are evenly matched in a drag race, with the 750-hp IQ trailing the 682-hp V by a couple tenths of a second to 60 mph (4.5 seconds to 4.3). On a rolling start from 5 mph, they’re tied at 4.7 seconds, and passing a dawdler will be a quicker task in the IQ: 50 to 70 mph in 2.6 seconds, versus 3.1 seconds for the loud Caddy.

Now, I’m old enough to remember the days when a Ferrari 458 Italia felt like it had pretty decent passing power, but the Escalade IQ’s 2.1-second 30-to-50-mph time beats the Ferrari by a tenth. If the IQ had been part of our Chariots of the Gods comparison test in 2011, its 50-to-70-mph time would have slotted it a tenth of a second behind the 458 and two-tenths ahead of the McLaren MP4-12C. Neither of those, when accelerating, heave their hoods skyward like a 70-foot Viking sportfish climbing on a plane, but the Escalade IQ certainly does. And they say EVs don’t bring any drama to the experience—behind the wheel, the IQ accelerating like my favorite Ferrari of all time feels as improbable as it actually is, like the vehicle itself is struggling to cope with its superpowers. An Escalade-V drives more like a mutant CT5-V Blackwing, and it’s definitely the Escalade where the driver’s seat is more desirable than one of the chauffeur chairs in back.

See all results for used cars for sale near 97754

If we accept that one of the primary goals for an Escalade is to project brash confidence, and that hugeness is fundamental to that project, the Escalade IQ wins going away. It reminds me of a scaled-up Rolls-Royce Cullinan in the side profile, and there’s a lot of side to behold: the IQ is more than a foot longer than the gas-powered Escalade. It’s also just newer, and I think it helps the premium aura not to have a bunch of Chevys and GMCs out there copping your style with very similar bodies. Also: rear-axle steering is unequivocally a flex, and the internal-combustion Escalade doesn’t have it.

Inside, both Escalades offer huge screens, lots of leather, and power-operated doors. However, only the IQ is eligible for the Curated by Cadillac program, wherein buyers fork over an additional $54,995 and get to choose from a huge palette of colors, inside and out. With 44 interior color choices, you can certainly spec a one-of-one IQ, while the Escalade-V sticks with “Yukon Denali, but fancier” as a guiding principle. Advantage, IQ.

When we’re discussing EVs versus internal combustion, range is usually an easy win for the gas-powered candidate. But not here—the Escalade-V has an EPA-rated 312 miles of range, while the IQ gets a 465-mile rating. Yes, the V will refuel a lot quicker, but you’ll be stopping way more often. And if your gas pump stops at $100, you might technically need to fill twice to top off the 24-gallon tank with premium.

Another factor I have to consider is rap music references, and on that front the Escalade-V is the easy winner, if only because the IQ is too new—most Escalade lyrics concern gas-powered models by default. Cadillac did hire Big Boi from Outkast to rap about the IQ, but that doesn’t count. (“Escalade IQ, this is the intro to, the all-electric Cadillac perfection ...”) I’m sure we’ll get there eventually: “Rolling in the IQ to the house with a view, IQ got the roof see-through, park the IQ next to the other ’cause I just bought two.”

Finally, I think part of the Escalade mystique has always hinged on leadership. This is a boss truck, from Sopranos to Succession. And where are the luxury winds blowing? It seems to me that in this space, which is to say “not a V-12 Ferrari,” the market is amenable to electrification and the technology is progressing fast. GM just announced that its forthcoming battery chemistry will improve range while being 20 percent more compact. There will come a time, maybe not far off, when EVs just make more sense objectively (they already do, depending on use case) and internal combustion will be the nostalgia play. So in this Would You Rather, I’m embracing that future and going for the Escalade IQ. The V is charming, but for $160,000, the IQ better fulfills the role of flagship SUV—quick, cocky, luxurious, and incontrovertibly baller.

Ezra Dyer is a Car and Driver senior editor and columnist. He's now based in North Carolina but still remembers how to turn right. He owns a 2009 GEM e4 and once drove 206 mph. Those facts are mutually exclusive.

14 Everyday Home Items You Need to Replace More Often Than You Think

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

These overlooked swaps can make your home cleaner, safer, and fresher.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Your home might be a showcase of timeless design, with stylish accessories, custom artwork, and high-end furniture. But when the everyday things that keep a household functioning are left unattended to for long enough, the space may be less clean and organized (and safe!) than it looks.

“Even the most gorgeous spaces lose their charm if we forget to update essentials,” says Linda Hayslett, principal designer at LH.Designs. “Keeping your home looking and feeling fresh isn’t just about design—it’s also about the little things.”

Caring for your home means looking beyond the interiors. Giving neglected areas the attention they need can keep germs and grime at bay, and replacing certain items can also protect your health and safety. Consider your carbon monoxide alarm: If an aging unit fails during an emergency, the consequences could be deadly.

To help you keep everything in good shape, we asked experts which 14 household items you should replace as soon as possible. Take a look—you can thank us later!

Smoke and carbon monoxide detectors lose their sensitivity over time, which could delay detection during an emergency. The timeframe for when you should replace these will vary by manufacturer. Look up the model you have online to be sure—even a fairly new alarm should be tested once a month. The batteries should be replaced once a year.

Mold and mildew can build up on shower liners, especially in damp environments. A 2024 study by SafeHome.org showed that shower curtains had the most germs of any other surfaces in the bathroom, including the toilet seat. While Hayslett recommends washing them every three months, the material can eventually become brittle over time.

Dingy towels are the antithesis of luxe. Towels lose their absorbency and softness after years of washing.

“Avoid using too much detergent, which can build up on fibers,” Hayslett says. “Use a little vinegar during the rinse cycle to keep towels soft.” If your bath towels feel scratchy or start to smell musty despite washing, it’s time for a refresh.

To prolong the life of your towels, make sure you let them dry properly between uses to prevent mildew growth, noted Vyanca Soto of Market Studio Interiors. And you should definitely swap them out if you notice discoloration from your skincare products.

You likely spend plenty of time in your kitchen and bathroom each day. Make sure you take a few minutes to examine the grout lines around your bathtub and kitchen countertops.

“Grout breaks down over time, and you can end up with water damage to the drywall,” says Rebekah Zaveloff, cofounder and creative director at Imparfait Design Studio. “If you look and there are spots that have pulled away or deteriorated, it’s time to regrout.”

A sagging mattress is a clear sign it’s time for a new one. Mattresses lose support and can accumulate dust mites, allergens, and even mold, which is certainly not a dreamy way to slumber.

“Flip your mattress every six months [unless it's a one-sided design] to help maintain its structure and extend its lifespan,” Hayslette advised.

A mattress protector can extend its lifespan as does regular vacuuming to remove dust and debris. Soto recently bought a bed vacuum cleaner and says, “It works like a charm.” She also uses it on her dog’s bed to help it stay clean and smell fresher.

The lifespan of a pillow mainly depends on its material, whether that be foam, feathers, or another type of synthetic fill. But once a pillow of any kind loses its shape, it's time to say goodbye. Like mattresses, pillows can harbor dust mites, dead skin cells, and sweat over time. You can prolong a pillow's life with a pillow protector—Soto likes the Serta Power Chill—but you should still swap those out every year or so.

When to replace: two to three years for sheets; five to 10 years for duvets

Bed linen care and personal hygiene dictate the condition of your linens. Similar to pillows, sheets and duvets can accumulate dust mites, allergens, and bacteria, especially if you allow kids or pets into your bed.

“Over time, duvets lose their fluffiness and insulating properties due to regular use and washing,” says Rebecca Bobroff of Rebecca Bobroff Design.

If you’re prone to night sweats or use a lot of skin-care products, you may notice that your sheets become quickly discolored. Along with regular washing, you may want to stick to white sheets you can bleach over colors, which will look dingy even when they're clean.

“At a minimum, wash pillowcases once a week,” says Danielle Hurhula, head of home merchandising for Quince. “I’d recommend bumping washes up to twice a week if you suffer from allergies or asthma, sleep with hair or skincare products, or have sensitive or acne-prone skin.”

HVAC maintenance experts, including Energy Star, recommend checking filters monthly and replacing air filters every three months to help your system function properly and keep dust and allergens out of your home.

If you remove your filters and they look extra dirty, you may want to up the frequency. Doing so will not only improve indoor quality but keep your HVAC system from overworking and wasting energy, which could shave a few years off its life.

Water filters can accumulate bacteria, minerals, and other contaminants. This can affect the taste and quality of your drinking water.

“The lifespan of a filter cartridge is influenced by the level of contaminants and particulates in your water,” says Eric Roy, Ph.D., founder and chief scientist at Hydroviv. “Sink filter cartridges should be replaced approximately every six months and are designed to handle up to 720 gallons of water.”

You should also watch for a decrease in the flow rate, which indicates the filter is saturated.

When to replace: five years (or as recommended by the manufacturer)

You likely know that fridge water filters should be replaced every six months, but water lines can build up gunk or start leaking over time.

“Inspect the line for cracks or discoloration every year,” Soto suggests. “Flush it with vinegar and water every six months to prevent mineral buildup and ensure clean, clear water. My mom taught me this and when you see the color of the water when you flush it—you'll never stop doing it.”

Using sharp knives on wooden and plastic cutting boards can create grooves that embed bacteria, even after a dishwasher cycle. One study showed that plastic surfaces have the longest bacteria survival time, and scraping wooden boards with a knife significantly reduced bacterial presence.

Soto disinfects cutting boards with a vinegar solution and uses bleach for plastic boards. “Sand down wooden boards occasionally to remove deep grooves and re-oil them to maintain the wood,” she says.

Sponges harbor bacteria, especially when you use them to clean up raw food such as eggs and meat. In fact, salmonella can thrive on a sponge for more than seven days. Hayslette’s tip: Microwave damp sponges for one minute to sanitize between uses, but toss them after two weeks max.

Keep sponges as dry as possible, or better yet, replace them with dish brushes as these are less likely to breed bacteria, according to the International Journal of Food Microbiology. We like the Curio dish brush because it stands upright on a magnetic pedestal and you can replace the brush heads.

When to replace: three to five years (or sooner if scratched)

The nonstick coating on your cookware can wear off or become damaged over time. This decreases a pan's efficacy and also makes it a health hazard—the coating may start to flake off into your food.

“To extend their life, always use nonmetal utensils and hand-wash your pans instead of placing them in the dishwasher,” Hayslette says.

Not only do latex anti-slip rug pads lose cushioning over time, but they can also start to disintegrate.

“They get tacky and sticky on the floor, which can damage the floor's surface,” Zaveloff explains. Pull up your rug once in a while and if you see that the rug pads are turning dark yellow, start shopping for new ones.

Disney Tried To Resurrect A Groundbreaking '60s Sitcom With A Movie That Bombed

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

In 1999, Disney decided it was time to bring back a 1960s sitcom about a lovable alien who shacks up with a newspaper reporter. It wasn't the most convincing reboot idea the studio ever had, but Disney pushed ahead with "My Favorite Martian" anyway. Unfortunately, the movie made just over half its budget back and has — like the sitcom on which it was based — been mostly forgotten.

"My Favorite Martian" became America's first fantasy sci-fi sitcom when it debuted in 1963, beating more well-known examples of the format such as "Bewitched" and "I Dream of Jeannie." "Mister Ed" had arrived two years prior and introduced the concept of a fantastical character being thrown into an otherwise standard sitcom setup (i.e. a talking horse living with an architect in his suburban home). But "My Favorite Martian" was the first sitcom to feature a human-looking being with fantastical powers. As such, it remains one of the most significant and quietly influential sitcoms in TV history.

The movie version, however, was neither significant nor influential. Disney brought in Christopher Lloyd and Jeff Daniels to lead this ill-advised reboot, which debuted in February 1999 and ended up making back just $36.8 million against a $65 million budget. What was the plan here? Well, "My Favorite Martian" was conceivably one of several '60s sci-fi shows that deserved a modern reboot. The problem was that the film wasn't any good.

The original version of "My Favorite Martian" is one of the best sci-fi sitcoms that takes place on Earth. It stars Bill Bixby as Los Angeles Sun reporter Tim O'Hara, who discovers the crashed spaceship of a 450-year-old Martian anthropologist (Ray Walston). With the alien's ship completely wrecked, Tim invites him to stay in his garage apartment, where the Martian quickly establishes himself as the wacky yet genial figure of "Uncle Martin." The pair agree to keep Uncle Martin's identity a secret while he tries to repair his ship, but they end up forming a bond that ultimately lasts for three seasons and 107 episodes.

"My Favorite Martian" episodes revolved around Uncle Martin's lack of familiarity with human customs, his incredible powers, and outlandish adventures that saw Tim and his alien pal using time machines and anti-gravity wristbands. The show also featured some esteemed guest stars, including a young Tom Skerritt who appeared in the 1963 episode "Mrs. Jekyll and Hyde." "My Favorite Martian" also welcomed "Gilligan's Island" star Alan Hale Jr., who appeared on an episode of the show the same year he set sail on his fateful voyage on the S.S. Minnow.

The sitcom came to an end in 1966, and 33 years later Disney decided it was time to give it another go. This time, however, the studio tried to reinvent the property as a family friendly big-screen adventure along the lines of "Flubber," which was itself based on a 1960s sci-fi comedy and had been distributed by Disney's Buena Vista Pictures in 1997. "My Favorite Martian," however, was nowhere near as successful as that Robin Williams-led comedy.

"My Favorite Martian" the movie is directed by Donald Petrie of "Mystic Pizza" and "Miss Congeniality" fame. It stars Jeff Daniels as Tim O'Hara, a news producer who, like in the original show, encounters a downed spacecraft while driving. In this version, however, the recently-fired O'Hara sees this discovery as a way to make it back into the news business. Christopher Lloyd plays The Martian, who follows Tim home and ends up staying. While Tim initially plans to expose his new friend and regain his job, he grows fond of Uncle Martin. When SETI starts hunting the pair, they have to work together to evade capture and get Uncle Martin back to his home planet.

Original Uncle Martin actor Ray Walston plays Armitan/Neenert in the film, a fellow Martian who became stranded on Earth years prior. This was a nice little full circle moment as it marked Walston's last theatrical film role prior to his death in 2001. Unfortunately, the film wasn't quite the tribute it could have been.

Aside from barely making half its budget back, "My Favorite Martian" earned a 14% critic score on Rotten Tomatoes. Kevin Thomas of the Los Angeles Times praised Walston for his "crisp wit and blithe sense of whimsy," which according to the critic was "otherwise lacking in this loser." Roger Ebert was slightly kinder, calling the movie "clever in its visuals," but "labored in its audios, and noisy enough to entertain kids up to a certain age." Otherwise the reviews were pretty damning, with Derek Adams of Time Out calling the film simply "Dire." Needless to say this was not one of the best sci-fi movies of 1999. You can see for yourself as the movie is available to watch on Disney+.

Tom Hanks’ $760M Mystery Thriller Is Officially Available to Stream for Free

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

He has covered everything from Marvel to the Oscars, and Marvel at the Oscars. He also writes obsessively about the box office, charting the many hits and misses that are released weekly, and how their commercial performance shapes public perception. In his time at Collider, he has also helped drive diversity by writing stories about the multiple Indian film industries, with a goal of introducing audiences to a whole new world of cinema.

After decades in the business, Tom Hanks has certainly found his audience and retained it. He offers them World War II movies, feel-good dramas, and even pulse-pounding potboilers. Among his most successful films is a mystery based on a bestselling novel. The movie was released two decades ago and was successful enough to launch a franchise. It's about to be rebooted, albeit without Hanks' involvement. The franchise's first installment was recently added to the library of a free streaming platform, just as its 20th-anniversary year comes to an end.

Hugely controversial at the time of its release, the movie's religious themes divided audiences much like a fellow blockbuster from two years earlier. In 2004, audiences around the world experienced Mel Gibson's The Passion of the Christ, which defied mixed reviews to gross more than $620 million worldwide. Hanks' film was released in 2006. It received worse reviews, but was a bigger hit, grossing around $760 million worldwide against a reported budget of $130 million.

The movie had an infamous premiere at the Cannes Film Festival, where it was reportedly met with jeers from certain sections of the audience. This divisiveness continued after the film's theatrical release, when some viewers questioned its historical accuracy. Hanks was shocked at the response; in his mind, the movie was nothing more than an entertaining thriller with little resemblance to reality. "Those are delightful scavenger hunts that are about as accurate to history as the James Bond movies are to espionage… All we were doing is promising a diversion," he told The New York Times in 2022.

A great detective is really just a personality with a method attached, which is why we keep remaking the same four of them. Here is yours, what it says about you, and the case for them being the best in the business.

You let people think you are not a threat and then you simply never go away. Columbo is the only major detective show that tells you who did it in the first ten minutes, because the pleasure was never the puzzle, it was watching a rich man slowly realise he is in trouble.

You are the most interesting person in the room and you know exactly how to use it. Rian Johnson built Blanc as a gentleman detective who is also an outsider everywhere he goes, which lets him be charming at people who would never otherwise talk to him.

Everybody tells you everything, and you have never once asked them to. Jessica Fletcher is a crime novelist from a small town in Maine who is so warm and so obviously trustworthy that suspects confess to her essentially as a social courtesy.

You expect to be lied to and you are almost never disappointed. Chandler wrote Marlowe as a man with a rigid private code in a city that has none, which is why he takes the case, solves it, gets nothing out of it and takes the next one.

We're talking, of course, about The Da Vinci Code, which Hanks called "hooey" in the same interview. The movie now holds a 25% score on the aggregator website Rotten Tomatoes, where the consensus reads, "What makes Dan Brown's novel a bestseller is evidently not present in this dull and bloated movie adaptation of The Da Vinci Code." The movie also featured Jean Reno, Audrey Tautou, Ian McKellen, Paul Bettany, and Alfred Molina.

The Da Vinci Code was added to the Pluto TV library this month, along with its two sequels, also starring Hanks in the lead role. Both follow-ups — Angels & Demons and Inferno — received poor reviews as well, and the franchise saw a decline in box-office returns. The series was rebooted as a television show that lasted one season on Peacock. A new reboot series is in development at Netflix.

Frontier Hires A Wizz Air Executive As Two Leaders Walk Out

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Frontier announced Monday that Michael Delehant will join as Executive Vice President and Chief Operating Officer (COO) on November 30, 2026 according to AirlineGeeks. He most recently ran operational and commercial activities as a group Managing Director at Wizz Air, and before that he was chief strategy and network officer at Vueling; he also held several leadership roles at Southwest. He will report to Frontier CEO, Jimmy Dempsey.

The other half of the announcement is the exits. Trevor Stedke, Senior Vice President of Operations, and Alex Clerc, Senior Vice President of Customers, will both depart on November 13. Frontier didn’t provide a reason for either. Not all departures have a “spending more time with my family” boilerplate announcement provided and there’s nothing wrong with that. But  it’s telling that the person running operations and the person in charge of customers are both gone, unironically about two weeks before a new operations boss shows up for work.

James Fenner, who has spent more than a decade at Frontier and was most recently Vice president of Revenue and Loyalty, becomes Senior Vice President and Chief Revenue Officer. His job now covers pricing, revenue management, loyalty and network planning. Most of those make sense to me together with the exception of loyalty. Pricing and revenue management go hand-in-hand as does network planning. But with the Frontier getting a dedicated first class, and the loss of Spirit (so far), Allegiant/Sun Country merging, and the struggles JetBlue is facing and Frontier’s own revenue challenges – it feels like loyalty should be its own gig.

Bobby Schroeter becomes Chief Marketing Officer, overseeing the brand and customer experience. With the customers executive leaving, customer experience moves under marketing. I would have thought loyalty responsibilities would have fallen to CMO but I am apparently mistaken.

Frontier lost $104 million in the Q3 2026, compared with a $77 million loss a year earlier. Removing special items and the airline made $40 million on a record $1.36 billion in revenue. Matthew covered it the day the numbers dropped as a loss despite record revenue, and he found reason for optimism.

Dempsey took over after longtime chief, Barry Biffle, left suddenly in December last year and was named CEO in January. This CEO, on the job about 10 months, is swapping out the operations chief, the customer chief and handing the revenue chief and in essence reinventing the carrier. I want to applaud Dempsey for taking his time to evaluate before making such dramatic changes. Newly installed leaders often want their own people and to break from the past to do things their way and make their own mark. However, as long as it has taken to make these changes, many might have presumed that he felt he was able to make those changes with the current staff in place.

Hiring a Wizz Air executive indicates what Frontier thinks its future playbook is. Delehant comes from two low-cost European carriers, Wizz and Vueling, and those two airlines operate differently from each other and the market is different from Frontier. Vueling is owned by International Airlines Group, the same parent as British Airways, Iberia, LEVEL, and Aer Lingus. Vueling operates outside of the network carrier model with connections and oneworld partner feed but still benefits from the incredible buying power of IAG. Wizz does not.

But for those who have called the death of LCCs and ULCCs in the United States with the shut down of Spirit, the new Frontier could fly in the face of that. Further, as pointed out on this site before, based solely on flying people and things, Ryan Air might be the most profitable true airline (not loyalty program) in the world. It closed last year earning $2.4bn, and every carrier that placed above Ryan had a loyalty program and credit card tie-in.

One thing that has become very clear, is that first class in the United States simply must be offered to be successful. Frontier, Allegiant/Sun Country, and JetBlue have all added a first class product this year (JetBlue had Mint for long haul but its shorter haul domestic first class is genuinely new.) Spirit had just moved into this space too, separating its Big Front Seat product into a First Class cabin, then European-style Business Class (blocked middle seat) though the writing was already on the wall in Fort Lauderdale.

These changes at Frontier could foretell the future of the ultra low cost model in the United States, first class and all.

Whether Delehunt’s changes have the capability of increasing Frontier Airlines stock price will likely determine just how much further he goes. But he’s right to install his own leadership, and given the market segment, quite savvy to find executives with experience in the ULCC space and at least some overlap in the United States in Southwest Airlines. Frontier has a credit card, its loyalty division now shares a boss with revenue management, and a hub model at least in part so it’s not a like-for-like fit with Wizz or other ULCC models. But it’s not a miss either, and I am interested in seeing just how much of the Spirit vacuum Frontier chooses to operate.

The 10 Most Perfect '90s Movie Movie Plot Twists, Ranked

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A New York native, Joe graduated from an avant-garde theater school upstate, where he was obsessed with sketch comedy. There, he and his lunatic friends founded The Sketchies — which still lives on at Skidmore College today. Living in New York City, a screenwriter friend of his then taught him how to write for film. This awakened a new passion in Joe. Since then his scripts have garnered over 200 festival selections, nominations, and wins all over the globe (including the BlueCat Best Feature award). Several of his screenplays have been made into feature films — some of them not that bad! Overall, he loves to create unique, hilarious, touching, and bizarre stories.

Oh, the 90s: what a time to be alive. The economy was booming, grunge music was flourishing, and just about every genre of film contributed at least one stunner with a glorious twist ending.

Obviously, this list is one giant string of spoilers — so, you are vehemently forewarned. Now, here are the most perfect movie plot twists of the 90s, a decade rife with intricately woven film devices. A lot of these flicks are in the thriller sphere, but there are also shocking gems that fall under drama, comedy, and action. Whichever way you classify these films, they all leave the viewer pleasantly head-spun.

Kicking off this list is a film that employs some major twists in a completely non-traditional way. This fantastically frenetic film begins with a very direct set-up, and then, almost out of nowhere, the action reverts, and the protagonist is given two different attempts to achieve her goal — all while German techno blasts, obviously.

Director/writer Tom Tykwer’s movie begins with the Berlin baddie Lola (Franka Potente) receiving a call from her distraught boyfriend, Manni (Moritz Bleibtreu). He’s lost 100,000 Deutsche Marks on the subway and if he doesn’t recover this loot in 20 minutes, his drug kingpin boss is going to kill him. So, Lola literally races around the city trying to somehow find this specific amount of cash. Her first attempt fails in dramatic fashion…and then the clock resets. In a full exploration of chaos theory, she starts her mad dash again, this time with an alternate plan. Again, massive fail. So, she gets one more try…which leads to the final twist. She’s finally successful in her third attempt, but when she meets Manni, in this timeline, he’s recovered the cash himself, and so the whole insane endeavor was for nothing. Ah, that’s German ingenuity for you.

Mm-hmm, redemption indeed. The Stephen King novella, Rita Hayworth and Shawshank Redemption, turned feature film is one of the best movies of the 90s, without question. Capturing not only the mood of the decades featured in the action (the 40s through the 60s), but also that of the prisoner mentality of the time, director Frank Darabont’s movie has lots of ups and downs, but no moment is as uplifting as the finale, which happens to contain quite the inventive twist.

Andy Dufresne (Tim Robbins) is an inmate in a penitentiary in Maine. He was wrongly accused of his wife’s murder and received a life sentence, but that doesn’t stop him from still trying to live his life to the fullest. His best buddy is the warmly wise Ellis Boyd 'Red' Redding (Morgan Freeman), and his nemesis is the Draconian, downright evil and corrupt Warden Norton (Bob Gunton). This man does everything in his power to suppress Andy’s seemingly indomitable spirit (along with those of all the other prisoners). Yet it’s Norton’s corruption that actually allows for Andy to have the last laugh. Andy systematically allocates the funds that Norton has pilfered and launders the cash — directing it to a bank account that only he can access under an alias (along with sending a newspaper a letter detailing all of Norton’s crimes against humanity). The ultimate twist comes when, after a routine cell inspection, it’s revealed that Andy is nowhere to be found. He spent the last twenty years chipping away at the wall of his cell (using a pin-up girl poster, of Rita Hayworth, to hide the hole) and digging a getaway tunnel to the sewer system — where he was able to eventually escape. The lesson, get busy livin’ or get busy dyin’, couldn’t be more heartfelt.

Writer-director Michael Haneke is known for being quite the provocateur. His viscerally brutal yet philosophically compelling film Funny Games vividly illustrates this point. While he remade it into a nearly identical American version, with the same title, the original German-language film was a bit sharper, all around.

Two teen boys, Peter (Frank Giering) and Paul (Arno Frisch), come to the vacation house of a small family: Georg (Ulrich Mühe), Anna (Susanne Lothar), and their little son, Schorschi (Stefan Clapczynski). The teens ask for some eggs, and it initially seems innocent enough. Then the one kid drops the eggs, repeatedly, and Anna’s and the audience’s Spidey-Senses begin tingling; something is rotten in the state of Austria. The teens then begin systematically breaking down this little family, injuring them and holding them hostage. When the unthinkable occurs (the kid is killed), the viewer now inherently believes that justice is coming, and the teens will pay, big time. Eventually, Anna frees herself and shoots Peter dead. Aha. Karmic retribution. Well, not quite. Paul takes the handy TV remote and — completely breaking the fourth wall — “rewinds” the action, so that Peter is still alive and now sees Anna’s move coming before she can do anything. What the what? The twist here is that nothing is real; it’s a movie, after all. Yet, Haneke raises a bigger question to the audience: you’re invested in the characters, but why? You know they are fictitious entities going into the viewing, so why should it shock you that they are given a bum deal/unfair outcome? No matter how one dissects this sick work of blasphemous art, it always leaves a nasty taste in one’s mouth…and that’s exactly the pleasantly putrid point.

While basically every decade since he hit the scene has been a good one for Brad Pitt, perhaps none showcased his range the ways the 90s did. One of his earliest, wildest roles takes place in the mind-bending Bruce Willis vehicle 12 Monkeys, directed by Terry Gilliam. While there aren’t any actual primates in the film, there is a lot of sci-fi monkey business going on, and the ending is truly bananas.

The plot of this film is crammed full of twisty turns, from the first act to the last. Set in 2035 (which eerily doesn’t seem that far off now…), most of humanity lives a subterranean life after a (man-made) virus has wiped out the majority of the world’s population. The good news is that some scientists have developed time travel; all they need is a test subject to go back in time to snag a pure sample of the early virus so they can create a cure, back (to the future) in the present. They pick a prisoner, James Cole (Willis), for the mission. What does he have to lose, right? It should be noted, the traumatized James has had a recurring dream since he was a mere boy; that of a man in an airport getting shot. Anyhoo, James goes back, appears to be a lunatic, links up with psychiatrist Dr. Kathryn Railly (Madeleine Stowe), and then ends up in a mental hospital where he encounters Jeffrey Goines (Pitt), an eco-warrior possibly responsible for the release of the nasty bug (he’s part of the “Army of 12 Monkeys,” which sounds less like an eco-terrorist group and more like an indie rock band). It’s not until the final sequence that it’s revealed that it was actually a deranged scientist, Dr. Peters (David Morse), who was responsible for the viral outbreak. James finds Peters in an airport, and before he can stop him, James is shot by airport police (he should’ve taken his shoes off before going through security), as a little boy watches this unfold — who turns out to be James himself (that’s where the dream came from). The swirly icing on this twisty cake is that once Peters boards a plane, en route to deploying the virus worldwide, the passenger next to him says her name is Jones (Carol Florence), and she’s “in insurance.” This final moment is a bit open-ended, but the ‘insurance’ quip seems to suggest she is there to get the pure virus sample, once and for all.

Most people that view this film are too busy trying to unpack all the metaphysical wackiness that occurred throughout the entire runtime to even let the bombshell ending twist really sit with them. While this hilarious Spike Jonze directed, Charlie Kaufman penned, work of sheer genius has wild turns throughout, its final mic drop of an ending is perhaps the film’s peak moment.

Craig Schwartz (John Cusack) is an out-of-work puppeteer, desperate to find work in his field. This is futile, even with all the support from his loving wife, Lotte (Cameron Diaz). Craig nabs a job at an odd company on the “7 and a half” floor of a building in NYC (in a nod to surrealist playwright Alfred Jarry, who lived in a “half floor” apartment in Paris, and also scribed the play Ubu Roi that was performed with puppets). Craig accidentally discovers an odd, fleshy hole behind a cabinet — that is a portal into the mind of the actor John Malkovich. So, Craig and his snarky co-worker Maxine Lund (Catherine Keener) open a covert business selling “rides” into Malko’s consciousness. Eventually, it’s revealed that Craig’s elderly boss, Dr. Lester (Orson Bean), has been readying himself and some senior citizen friends to enter the portal together and take over Malkovich’s body forever. There’s a lot more to this, but, basically, Craig wants to use Malkovich’s fame and money to lock down Maxine and enters the portal too late. He’s diverted to the subconscious of Maxine’s new baby — who Maxine raises with Lotte — where Craig is a silent prisoner, forever. It’s all quite heady.

Some games are fun. Some games are exciting. And then there’s The Game, which may or may not cause the player to intentionally plummet to their apparent demise. Master of the shadowy shot, David Fincher, directs this masterwork in manipulative mind games and, wow, is it ever decadently dark.

Nicholas Van Orton (Michael Douglas) is a rich dude who is so accustomed to having everything in life that basically nothing excites him anymore. His freewheeling brother, Conrad (Sean Penn), gets him a gift for his birthday, simply called “The Game.” Before Nicholas even realizes it, he’s playing. It’s just, this game is so exhilarating and disruptive, it seems that it’s veered off course and Nicholas’s life is actually in grave danger. After a series of incidents that are beyond harrowing, Nick finds himself on the roof of a very tall building, his entire fortune having been wiped clean, apparently being hunted by a vast network of high criminals — with seemingly no way out. He opts to jump, ostensibly ending his life…only there’s a big suicide prevention balloon underneath him. This was all part of the game, as Conrad and other birthday party guests pop out and congratulate Nicholas on a well-played adventure. Hmm. Fun.

“What’s in the box?” Ah, a question for the ages. David Fincher’s pitch black, bleak, awful thriller is one of the most satisfyingly perverse films ever produced. With a dark mystery insidiously pulsing through the whole film, this wonderfully waterlogged movie is thrilling throughout, and culminates in a twist of pure, heartless beauty.

Detective Mills (Brad Pitt) is a newbie cop in town, whereas Detective Somerset (Morgan Freeman) is a veteran, on the verge of retirement. When a string of sick murders/assaults occur which appear to be under the religious umbrella of the seven deadly sins, the two gumshoes know they’re in for a wild ride of a case. As they try to home in on the culprit (before the seventh crime is committed/enacted), the alleged perpetrator, John Doe (Kevin Spacey, at his creepiest), turns himself in. It’s a baffling move, but he calmly croons that he will lead the detectives to the final crime site if they accompany him to a yet undisclosed location, alone. They comply, and that’s when it gets really interesting. A clueless delivery dude shows up with a box, meeting John, Mills, and Somerset, in a remote part of the Californian desert. Well, the box contains the head of Mill’s wife, Tracy (Gwyneth Paltrow). John explains that his own personal sin was “envy,” as he envied Mill’s nice, normal, wife-having life. The final sin left to commit is wrath…which Mills, between bitter tears, executes, putting a bullet in John’s head. It’s an ironic ending; John Doe, the villain, wins — even though his brains get blown out. All in all, a mind-blowing experience…for the character and the audience.

Who is Keyser Söze? That’s the main question at the heart of director Bryan Singer’s crime-action-thiller-mystery The Usual Suspects. With one of the most well-crafted scripts of the decade, not to mention searingly stellar performances, brilliant direction, sharp cinematography, and crisp editing, this film hits all the right notes throughout its symphonic runtime. And, naturally, it concludes with one of the most jaw-dropping, and coolest, twists in movie history.

The film is framed with a very nifty device; a low-level career criminal, Verbal (Kevin Spacey, having a banner year), is interrogated by the bullish, blowhard detective Dave Kujan (Chazz Palminteri), in a precinct room. The loquacious Verbal, who has a disability that impedes his arm and leg from functioning normally, reluctantly provides Kujan with info surrounding a boat heist gone wrong. Verbal and a deliciously diverse cadre of crime types, including the clownish McManus (Stephen Baldwin), the wisecracking Hockney (Kevin Pollak), and mumbling Fenster (Benicio Del Toro), were given faulty information and most of them ended up dead during the botched robbery. Kujan presses Verbal about the de facto leader of the group, Keaton (Gabriel Byrne), accusing him of being the notorious Söze. Eventually, Verbal is released, and that’s when it hits Kujan: Verbal was giving an Upright Citizen’s Brigade-level improv class, as he took random words, names, and phrases from around the office and spun them into a long-winded yarn about the whole criminal endeavor. As Verbal loses his limp and lights a cig, we collectively realize that he was the lethal rapscallion Keyser all along. Mind. Blown.

The first rule of Fight Club may be that you don’t talk about Fight Club, but here, we simply must. The phenomenal Chuck Palahniuk novel turned David Fincher film begins with the explosion of the unnamed protagonist’s condo, and never lets its filmic foot off the gas. Yes, this movie is the physical manifestation of octane fuel mixed with 100-proof testosterone, but there’s oh so much more to it than just boys masquerading as manly men.

The Narrator (Edward Norton) is a milquetoast dude, working a boring job, living an Ikea-inspired life. He has chronic insomnia that eventually becomes a major factor in the twist ending. He begins going to nighttime support groups (for afflictions that he doesn’t have). Here he meets Marla Singer (Helena Bonham Carter), a tsunami of disaster in cool sunglasses. Also, during his travels, he encounters what can only be described as his polar opposite: Tyler Durden (Brad Pitt), the badass artisanal soap entrepreneur who is everything the Narrator wishes he could be. After starting an underground fight club, which blossoms into a national movement of chaos and calculated destruction, the Narrator eventually comes to a disturbing conclusion: he is Tyler. The real fly in the ointment is that Tyler wants him dead, and seems pretty intent on making this happen (along with blowing up all the financial institutions in the city). Probably the most entertaining examination of multiple personality disorder caught on celluloid, Fincher’s darkly comedic thriller has one of the coolest twist endings ever — accompanied by the perfect Pixies song.

Unless you’ve been living under a rock, that’s buried under a boulder, that’s housed in an end-of-the-world bunker, you probably already know the brain-exploding twist that drops at the end of The Sixth Sense. If not, whoo-hoo, are you in for one sensationally spooky spoiler (even comic Nate Bargatze made fun of people who don’t know the end of this movie by now). Seriously, if you don’t know what the big reveal is, stop reading this article immediately, find auteur creator M. Night Shyamalan’s supernatural mystery streaming somewhere, and watch the heck out of it.

Cole Sear (Haley Joel Osment) is a boy with a unique gift that actually is much more of a curse: he sees dead people (as the now infamous lines goes). He connects with a child therapist, Malcolm Crowe (Bruce Willis), who has a knack for helping disturbed children, even if his track record is not exactly perfect (one of his former patients, Vincent Gray (Donnie Wahlberg), shot him). So, Malcolm spends the majority of the film trying to help Cole navigate the unsettling world of the undead…only to finally realize that he is a ghost as well. It’s probably the most perfectly executed twist in cinema history, but hey, that’s just the opinion…of basically everyone.

Marriott Bonvoy Boundless Card Review: Worth The $95 Fee For The Free Night

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Marriott has co-branded credit cards issued by both American Express and Chase, and it can be tough to figure out the differences between these cards. This is my review of the most popular mid-range Marriott credit card in that portfolio, the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card. It’s a card that many Marriott guests will likely want to have, if eligible.

Let’s dive into the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless Card’s details to see whether it’s worth it and who it makes the most sense for.

Link: Learn more about the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card

Before I get into my full Marriott Bonvoy Boundless Card review, here’s a brief look at the card’s key details:

I also put together my list of pros and cons for the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless Card:

To start, let’s take a look at the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless Card welcome bonus, card eligibility rules, and fees. After all, these are important factors that cardmembers consider when applying for cards. Now is an especially good time to apply, thanks to the current offer.

The Marriott Bonvoy Boundless Card is currently offering a multi-part welcome offer, whereby you can earn three free night awards. The bonus is broken down as follows:

Based on my latest miles & points valuations, I consider Bonvoy points to be worth 0.7 cents each, given all the great ways there are to redeem them. While the maximum value of this offer is 150,000 points, I wouldn’t value the bonus quite that high, given the limited flexibility (you need to redeem the free night award within a year, you’re capped at properties costing 50,000 points, etc.).

However, if it were worth 150,000 points, I’d consider that to have a value of $1,050, and that doesn’t factor in the potential airline statement credits.

If you’re applying for the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless Card, the general Chase application restrictions apply. There aren’t too many direct, consistent rules, but considerations include Chase’s 5/24 rule (which isn’t always enforced), being able to get up to two Chase cards every 30 days, etc.

Beyond that, since both American Express and Chase issue cards for Marriott, there are some pretty complicated restrictions regarding eligibility for this card, so see this post for more details.

To summarize the very basics, you can’t get the welcome bonus on the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless Card if:

The Marriott Bonvoy Boundless Card has a $95 annual fee. You can add authorized users at no extra cost. That’s an incredibly reasonable annual fee, given the amount of annual fee inflation we’ve seen over the years on other cards.

The Bonvoy Boundless Card has no foreign transaction fees, so it’s an excellent card for purchases abroad. That means you can earn rewards on your foreign spending, without having to pay any fees that will offset those rewards. In particular, it’s a good card to use for foreign Marriott spending.

While I wouldn’t recommend using the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless Card for your everyday spending, there are potentially some situations where it could make sense. Let’s go over the rewards structure of the card, including the bonus categories and other incentives to spend.

The Marriott Bonvoy Boundless Card offers 6x Bonvoy points for spending at Marriott-family hotels globally. For many people, this will be a worthwhile bonus.

Since I value Bonvoy points at 0.7 cents each, that’s the equivalent of a 4.2% return on hotel spending. While there are other great cards for hotel spending, some people will want to use this as their card for Marriott spending.

Keep in mind this is on top of the standard points that you can earn for Marriott Bonvoy hotel stays. At most brands, Bonvoy members earn 10x points per dollar spent, and that’s before elite bonuses, which range from 10-75%. So as a Bonvoy Silver member paying with the card (the minimum status you get with this card), you’re looking at earning 17x points per dollar spent at most brands, minimum.

The Marriott Bonvoy Boundless Card offers 3x Bonvoy points on dining, gas station, and grocery store purchases globally. This only applies to the first $6,000 spent on combined purchases in those categories annually, and after that, you’ll earn 2x Bonvoy points on those purchases.

Since I value Bonvoy points at 0.7 cents each, the 3x points is like the equivalent of a 2.1% return on eligible gas, grocery, and dining spending. While there are still more rewarding cards for spending in the categories of dining, gas stations, and groceries, this is about as good of a rewards structure as you’ll see on a Bonvoy credit card.

The Bonvoy Boundless Card offers 2x Bonvoy points on all other eligible purchases. I don’t consider 2x points per dollar spent to be especially compelling since I value that at a return of 1.4 cents per dollar of spending, and there are better cards for everyday spending.

How would I approach the value of Marriott points? As mentioned above, based on my valuations, I consider Bonvoy points to be worth 0.7 cents each. I’m not going to suggest that Bonvoy points are my favorite points currency — they’re not — but the reality is that Marriott has a huge global portfolio, and many of the properties are quite decent.

Generally you’ll find that Bonvoy award stays cost anywhere from 10,000 to 200,000 points per night, though some hotels do fall outside of that range. You can maximize value by taking advantage of the fifth night free benefit, which is open to elite members.

This is an extreme example of value, but take Hotel Cala di Volpe, a Marriott Luxury Collection hotel in Sardinia, which is the brand’s most expensive property in summer. I just pulled up a five night redemption (including a fifth night free) for 776,000 Bonvoy points. By my valuation, that’s like $5,432 worth of points.

Meanwhile the cash rate at the hotel is 4,161 EUR ($4,639) per night, so those same five nights would cost you $23,195. Talk about huge savings!

One unique thing about Bonvoy points is they can also fairly efficiently be converted into airline miles. You can transfer your rewards to dozens of different airline programs, and a vast majority allow a 3:1 transfer ratio. On top of that, you get a bonus of 5,000 airline miles for every 60,000 Bonvoy points you transfer. In other words, with most programs you can convert 60,000 Bonvoy points into 25,000 airline miles, which is potentially a decent value.

For more details, see my guide to redeeming Bonvoy points.

One of the primary reasons to consider getting the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless Card is how the card can help you earn elite status in the Bonvoy program. Status starts to get valuable around the Bonvoy Platinum tier, when you become eligible for perks like complimentary breakfast and valuable room upgrades.

One of my favorite perks of having the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless Card is that you get 15 elite nights toward status annually just for having the card.

Note that several co-branded Marriott credit cards offer this, and you can only receive the benefit once per year on a personal card. In other words, if you have three Marriott personal cards, then you wouldn’t get 45 elite nights.

However, you could earn 15 elite nights from this personal card and 15 elite nights from a business card, so that you’d earn 30 elite nights in a year. The ideal setup would be to have a personal card in conjunction with the Marriott Bonvoy Business® American Express® Card (learn more).

This makes it more attainable to earn Platinum status, which requires 50 elite nights. If you had the Bonvoy Boundless Card, you’d only need 35 more elite nights, and if you also had the business card you’d only need 20 more elite nights.

Note that if you really want to maximize elite nights with personal credit cards, the Marriott Bonvoy Brilliant® American Express® Card (learn more) offers 25 elite nights per year, which is the most elite nights you’ll get with any Marriott credit card.

The Marriott Bonvoy Boundless Card offers one elite night for every $5,000 spent on the card. Any elite nights earned this way will be credited to your account within eight weeks of the last day of the calendar month in which you qualify.

Personally, I think spending $5,000 to earn one elite night is a lot, but everyone’s situation will differ, and for some people, this could make sense if you’re otherwise close to earning status. This is the only Marriott credit card that offers bonus elite nights for spending.

Perhaps this is a bit redundant, but just for having the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless Card you receive Silver elite status in the Bonvoy program. Now, don’t get too excited, as this doesn’t exactly come with that many benefits.

Marriott Silver elite status ordinarily requires 10 elite nights, and comes with perks like 10% bonus points on hotel stays, priority late check-out, and free Wi-Fi. So it’s better than nothing, but that’s about all I can say.

If you have the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless Card, you can earn Marriott Gold elite status if you spend $35,000 on the card in a calendar year. This status ordinarily requires 25 elite nights. Bonvoy Gold elite status gets you benefits like 25% bonus points on hotel stays, 2PM late check-out, a room upgrade subject to availability, and more.

In addition to the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless Card spending bonus categories and ability to earn elite status, there are some other major perks to having the card. This is where the card’s annual fee becomes really easy to justify, in my opinion.

The real perk that makes the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless Card worthwhile is that you get a free night award every year on your account anniversary, valid for a one-night hotel stay at a property costing up to 35,000 points.

It’s also possible to top off Marriott free night awards with up to 25,000 additional points to redeem at more expensive properties. This means you could redeem a free night award plus 25,000 points to stay at a hotel costing 60,000 points per night.

This is an exceptional deal, and I think this card is worth having. Marriott is the world’s largest hotel chain, so regardless of your preferred hotel program, just about everyone may find themselves in a situation where this could come in handy.

I consistently redeem this certificate at hotels costing $250+ per night. Based on my valuation of 0.7 cents per point, I’d consider the free night award to be worth around $245, more than double the annual fee… that’s awesome!

The Marriott Bonvoy Boundless Card offers auto rental collision damage waiver coverage. Decline the rental company’s collision insurance and charge the entire rental cost to your card.

Coverage is provided for theft and collision damage for most cars in the United States and abroad. Do note that within the United States, coverage is secondary to your personal insurance, though. Of course you’ll always want to check the guide to benefits for all the details.

The Marriott Bonvoy Boundless Card offers a variety of protection when traveling. Among these features are:

Make sure you check your cardmember agreement and guide to benefits for all of the details, since there are terms to be aware of.

Personally, the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless Card isn’t a card that I would be using for my everyday spending. However, I think the card is worth having, given that it offers a free night certificate on your account anniversary every year, which should more than justify the annual fee.

If you’re going for status, the 15 elite nights toward status annually is incredibly valuable as well, and makes it easier to earn Platinum status.

I think there’s absolutely merit to picking up the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless Card, though I’d say that the Marriott Bonvoy Brilliant® American Express® Card (learn more) might be the best alternative to consider. While the card is issued by American Express and has a steep $650 annual fee (Rates & Fees), there are lots of reasons to consider this card, including:

Obviously this card won’t be for everyone, and the fee is steep. However, I do think the perks add up, and if you’re involved quite a bit in the Bonvoy ecosystem, it’s easy to make the math on the card work.

If you’re not actually that committed to the Marriott Bonvoy ecosystem, there are lots of other great cards to consider. In particular, these are cards with relatively reasonable annual fees and great rewards structures, and they’re very well rounded:

The Marriott Bonvoy Boundless Card is a great card with a reasonable annual fee, and offers an annual free night award and 15 elite nights toward status annually. This is an especially good time to apply, given the welcome offer that’s currently available.

For those looking to earn Bonvoy points, this is also one of Marriott’s better credit cards for being rewarded, between the points and additional elite nights. However, this still isn’t the card I’d be used for my everyday spending. Instead, this is a card you get for the perks it offers, as it can make sense to hold onto this card even with minimal spending.

The Best Energy Stock to Buy With Oil Near $105 Isn't an Oil Producer

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Oil prices are high today, but also highly volatile; you can sidestep the commodity risk with this high-yield energy investment.

Enterprise Products Partners (EPD +1.12%) operates in the energy sector, but oil prices aren't the main determinant of its success. That's an important distinction today as you consider energy stocks, with oil prices trading around $105 per barrel. It wasn't too long ago that oil was less than half that. Here's how Enterprise avoids the commodity risk that buying an energy producer would expose you to.

The broader energy sector is generally broken into three parts: the upstream (production), the midstream (pipelines), and the downstream (chemicals and refining). The upstream and the downstream are heavily impacted by commodity prices. The midstream simply connects the upstream to the downstream and the rest of the world. Midstream businesses like Enterprise charge fees for the use of their energy infrastructure assets, so the volume flowing through their systems is more important than the price of what is being moved.

Compared to the upstream and the downstream, the midstream is actually pretty boring. And Enterprise is one of the most boring midstream operators around. While it is among the largest pipeline businesses in North America, it has long focused on slow and steady growth. That's highlighted in its distribution, which has increased annually for 28 years. That's basically as long as it has been publicly traded.

Distribution growth tends to be in the low- to mid-single digits, but slow and steady is hard to complain about in a sector known for volatility. Meanwhile, Enterprise has an investment-grade-rated balance sheet, and its distributable cash flow covered its distribution by 1.7x in 2025. There is material room for adversity before a distribution cut would be considered here. And it has $6.5 billion in capital investment planned through 2029, so more slow-and-steady growth is a far more likely outcome.

All that said, the single factor likely to be most interesting to investors is Enterprise's roughly 6% distribution yield. That's six times what you would get from the broader market today. While upstream and downstream investments rise and fall along with oil prices, you can comfortably sit back and collect a well-above-market yield that has grown steadily for decades. If history is any guide, oil prices will eventually fall from their lofty levels after the geopolitical conflict in the Middle East ends. If you own Enterprise, you probably won't care all that much if they do.

Reuben Gregg Brewer has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool recommends Enterprise Products Partners. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Using miles to fly for Thanksgiving? These are the most expensive and cheapest routes

📅 2026-11-08  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Airline ticket prices have surged this year, especially for travel over the holidays. Don't expect many bargains if you're booking flights with miles, either.

Tickets bought with frequent flyer miles for flights between large U.S. airports over the Thanksgiving travel period will cost 32% more than last year's holiday, according to data from Seats.aero, a miles and points redemption search site. While fares have skyrocketed this year, airline executives last month said demand remains strong.

Airlines have largely gotten rid of static mileage redemption charts in favor of dynamic pricing, making budgeting for an award ticket more difficult. Dynamic pricing means redemption costs change depending on demand, routes and other factors.

Many of the most expensive routes this Thanksgiving are understandably longer, including from Hawaii, but the list also includes a nonstop from New York to Houston.

Here's how mileage redemptions for the holiday are stacking up, based on one-way flights on average as of early November and compared with last year:

Jet fuel prices have surged since the U.S. and Israel attacked Iran on Feb. 28, which has eaten into airline profits. Carriers have sought to pass costs along to consumers as much as possible.

Even some of the cheapest routes for award tickets are more expensive than last year, including for short routes like Tulsa, Oklahoma, to Dallas Fort Worth International Airport.
