Over Pinchot Pass — John Muir Trail Day 10

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The night before, at Rae Lake, I ate some kind of buffalo stew, which was supposed to provide about 900 calories.  It is funny how flavor (usually) takes a backseat to caloric value on the trail.  I am just so conscious that I am exerting myself — a lot — and need to maximize my fuel intake.

After dinner I had my headlamp on in the tent, probably dictating my notes, and was not aware that Evelyn (or Serena?) was taking pictures of my tent in the darkness under a starry sky.

Night camping at Rae Lakes (photo courtesy of Evelyn or Serena)

My sleep started deep and sound.  Then I did wake up, coughing a bit, and I thought, “Oh, no!”  But it was still much less than before, and I was very tired, so I slept again.  I vaguely remember dreaming about how some people were organizing something-or-other and I was confused how it was going to work.  Maybe it meant I was wondering how to manage this journey?

Around 4:40 in the morning, when I got up to pee, I checked the Garmin and saw the time.  I thought, “Well, I think I’m not going to go back to sleep.”  So I didn’t; instead I fetched my food from the bear box, and ate my breakfast.  But I felt sluggish, like I was swimming through molasses.

How sluggish?  Well, I rose at 4:40 but I did not get on the trail until 7:00 a.m.  Despite everything, it was only thirty minutes later than the previous day, but it was far from efficient.  I said goodbye to Evelyn and Serena; they were heading southbound, and I was heading northbound.  I left the crowded campsite and started on my way.

One consequence of the Rae Lake crowds is that it was hard to find a private place to dig a cathole.  I decided to wait for a better place.  About a half hour down the trail, I pulled off and found a spot that was quite lovely and dug my hole and did my business.  In the middle of it, a deer appeared in the meadow next to me and gazed at me curiously.

Cat hole in the foreground, amazing view in the background!

Less than a half mile further, I discovered a beautiful camping site with a bear box and no crowds at all.  I thought, “Oh my gosh, if I’d just known and been willing to take the risk to go a little further yesterday, it would have been a much nicer situation.”  It was too late for me, but I resolved to tell any southbound hikers I encountered that they would be more comfortable stopping at the last camp site before the main Rae Lake location.

More beautiful and fewer crowds — the place to be!

As I looked at the long day ahead of me, I realized it would be 6-7 miles, all downhill, followed by 8-9 miles all uphill, followed by a mile or two of downhill to my next camp site at Lake Marjorie.  In elevation I started at 10,500 feet, would drop to 8,500 feet, and then would cross Pinchot Pass at 12,200 feet.  I planned for a long day.

I did about four miles on that nice downhill to start the day when I encountered a small stream I had to cross.  In general, these stream crossings had not been too challenging, frankly.  I brought my water shoes explicitly because I thought I would wade through river crossings, perhaps as much a thigh-deep, and there hadn’t been any like that – at least so far.

This one was no different, a two-stone hop across with a couple of different options as to route.  That, it turned out, was the problem.  As I prepared to take my second step I decided to go via a different sequence of stones, and I turned slightly to change direction.  It was a slight turn, nothing notable about it at all.  Except… Suddenly I felt a sharp pain in my left knee.

I let out a yowl and finished crossing.  But I was worried.  Clearly, I had injured something. The only question was, what was it and how badly was it damaged?

I was still able to move it, so that was a good first sign.  Working it gently, it barked at me in certain angles; but just going straight ahead, it seemed not to bother me too much.

I decided to take an Ibuprofen at the bottom of the hill to prevent swelling from whatever it was that had happened.  Five minutes later I changed my mind, thinking, “Why am I waiting  to take the medication?  I just sustained the injury.  I should take the ibuprofen immediately, right NOW.”  And I did.

I continued down gingerly, favoring my knee a bit, worried that this might be something serious, and not knowing whether it would be or not.

My main distraction was lizards.  Yes!  I had observed no reptiles at all above 10,000 feet, but coming lower they started to appear.  I think they were most likely Western Fence Lizards, but they could also have been Sagebrush Lizards.  Because of my focus on my knee, I did not photograph them, but I resolved to do so the next time they appeared.

Finally, I reached the bottom of the downhill.  There was a suspension bridge to cross there, and it was one of the bouncier ones I had ever been on.  With a GoPro I would gladly have filmed myself crossing it, but with my hand-held phone it was too easy to imagine it flying out of my hand into the rushing waters below.

From solid ground I took a couple of pictures of the bridge anyway.

Then I crossed to the other side and began the big uphill.  I was facing around 3,600 feet of elevation gain, while worrying about my stupid knee.  I also worried that the trail might feel kind of endless, and it did feel kind of endless.

Along the way I started leapfrogging another hiker named Amy.  Our hiking styles almost made it inevitable.  I would go fast but then stop and rest; she kept a steady pace.  So, I would go by her moving faster, and she would move by me when I stopped.  Then I would resume and pass her again, and the cycle would continue.  At one point, when I was ahead, I saw a grouse.  I wondered if it would still be there when she came by.

The good news in all of this was that either the Ibuprofen was working, or my knee was not badly injured, or both.

Amy and I had both set Lake Marjorie, on the other side of Pinchot Pass, as our destination for the night.  This was the first camping space about a mile and a half below the summit.

We kept ascending.  The one advantage was that the ascent took place over many miles, so it was rarely going up very steeply.  But over the course of the afternoon, it was feeling quite relentless.  Only the series of waterfalls along the way kept me engaged and appreciative.

…another waterfall falling in perpetuity!

Approaching Pinchot Pass I ran into a guy coming downhill toward me.  We chatted and I learned that, like me, he was 69 years old.  He seemed very demoralized.  He said he felt like he was shuffling, like he could barely even do it.  He said he was planning to get off at Kearsarge Pass and probably would not return.  I said, “Well, what you should do is get off at Kearsarge and go to Independence and then spend a day there doing nothing.  After a day off trail, your mind will be clearer, and then you can decide if that’s the end for you, or if you do actually want to come back.”  That was my recommendation; I don’t know if he took it.

Nearing the top, there were many rocks patterned with white striations.  I wanted to turn to Sam and ask the geologist to explain what had caused them.  But of course, he was back in Boulder by then.

You can see the white stripes in the foreground rocks, but also in the background cliff face. Quartz maybe? Sam could have told me…

The end of the trail up to the pass was a series of switchbacks.  You couldn’t really see where they were leading until, suddenly, ta-da!, you had reached the saddle of the pass.

It was a really, really hard day.  I started at seven a.m. and I got to the pass at exactly five p.m.  If you do the math, that’s ten hours.  And I still had the mile and a half to go down to the lake.

The views over the lakes as the sun was setting were beautiful.  Amy finally caught up to me again, at a stream where I stopped to top off my water supply before making camp.  That late in the afternoon, we were both worried that the camping spots might already be filled up.  But when I saw a truly beautiful location, on nice flat sandy ground, I said, “That’s the one for me!”  Amy kept going to the next spot further down the trail, but I called it a day.

Arriving at Rae Lake the previous day, I was so tired it was difficult to function.  On this occasion, at Lake Marjorie, I set up calmly and reasonably efficiently, and it just felt much more natural than it had up to that point.  I pitched my tent, soaked my food, and set up my sleeping system, of course.  But I also went down to the lake shore where I washed my socks and washed my feet.  While I had my feet in the lake’s icy cold water, I thought, “Oh, I need to ice my knee too.”  So, I went a little deeper into the lake and got my knee in the icy cold water as well.

Unfortunately, my dinner was disgusting.  Some of these freeze-dried meals work great for a cold-soaker, but some really need boiling water to be edible.  I just wish I had kept track of which was which for future hikes!  Dinner was the low point of the day (other than my knee tweak), but I ate it for the calories.

Early Evening by Lake Marjorie. Notice my tent to the right of the boulder and — at that point — nothing to the left of the boulder.

Sitting in my tent pitched next to a large boulder it was getting colder-and-colder at our high elevation.  It was definitely a night for my sleeping bag liner!  In deep twilight I heard a large animal moving around on the other side of the big boulder.  Initially I was quite spooked, but when I checked I saw that a late-arriving hiker was setting up camp there.

For a moment I was annoyed that my delicious privacy had been disturbed.  Then I overcame that feeling and went over to welcome the newcomer and see if they needed anything.  It was a solo hiker named Sally, born in China but now an Australian citizen, also NOBO on the John Muir Trail.  She was friendly and we chatted for a few moments before I returned to my tent to call it a night.

It had been a 17-mile day, my longest yet on the trail.  Sleep came easily.

Hi, I’m Papa George! In my 69 years I’ve done a lot of cool things – I’ve crossed the Atlantic in a 40-foot sailboat, bushwacked 100 miles across the north slope of the Alaska Range, hiked for 26 days in Western Nepal crossing five passes over 5,000 meters, and climbed Mt. Kilimanjaro, to name a few. It was my honor to serve my country as a diplomat around the world for 34 years. I have been active in retirement, and I hiked the Wonderland Trail in 2024 and the Long Trail in 2025. This year I'm trying... the John Muir Trail!

Modern life is changing the DNA of bacteria in our guts

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Gut bacteria can acquire genes from neighboring microbes, adding to the genetic differences linked to how people live.

Diet, antibiotics, and indoor plumbing help shape which bacteria settle into a person’s gut. That much has been clear for years.

What researchers couldn’t say is whether the bacteria themselves have been evolving in response.

A study of nearly 6,000 gut bacterial genomes finds that they have.

Across the species examined, strains from industrialized communities carried more genes than those from non-industrialized communities.

Most of the extra genes arrived recently. The bacteria didn’t inherit them from a parent cell. They took them from other microbes living alongside them.

So modern life hasn’t only changed which bacteria live in the gut. The same species now carries a different set of genes depending on how its host lives.

Researchers at Kiel University’s Institute of Clinical Molecular Biology and MIT’s Department of Biological Engineering ran the work, with the evolutionary modeling done in Japan.

Everything here was measured in bacterial genomes, so none of it shows that a bigger bacterial genome makes a person healthier or sicker.

All of the genomes come from the Global Microbiome Conservancy, a non-profit effort to bank living strains from communities that microbiome research has mostly passed over.

For this study, 20 volunteers in Cameroon, Ghana, Malaysia, Nigeria and Rwanda gave fresh stool samples. None had taken antibiotics recently.

Scientists processed each sample on site within three hours. The samples were then frozen in liquid nitrogen at minus 321°F (minus 196°C).

In the lab, the material went onto plates inside an oxygen-free chamber. Colonies grew at 99°F (37°C) for one to two weeks. Single colonies were then picked and sequenced.

The cultures yielded 1,841 new bacterial genomes. Added to 4,140 strains already banked, the collection reached 5,981 genomes. They came from 56 donors in nine countries.

Researchers classified donors as industrialized or non-industrialized using a development score for where they lived, compared with their country’s median.

From that collection, the team picked ten species sampled well on both sides of the divide, with at least eight strains from each side. Most are Bacteroides and close relatives, the workhorses of the lower gut.

E. coli was left out on purpose. Its genetic diversity has already been mapped in detail.

In all ten species, strains from industrialized guts carried more protein-coding genes. The difference was statistically significant in eight of the ten species.

Core genes, the ones every strain of a species has, showed no difference. The extra material was all optional genes, the kind one strain carries and the next one doesn’t.

The optional sets also varied more from strain to strain in industrialized hosts, again in all ten species. The team then widened the comparison to 34 species and saw the same pattern.

A bigger genome could mean two different things. Either these strains picked up genes lately, or strains that already had big genomes happened to do well in modern guts.

Separating the two fell to a group in Okinawa, Japan. They compared the family tree of a single gene with the family tree of the species carrying it.

A mismatch between the two marks the spot where a gene was gained, lost or handed over.

Gene gains clustered near the tips of the evolutionary trees, indicating that they occurred relatively recently.

They also piled up on the branches belonging to industrialized hosts.

Between 80% and 90% of those gains were transfers from other microbes. Only a small share came down from a parent cell.

Many arrived packaged inside plasmids and viruses that infect bacteria.

Both deliver several genes at once. Swapping of this kind goes on well beyond the gut, in soil, seawater and thawing permafrost.

Gergely Szöllősi heads the Model-Based Evolutionary Genomics Unit at the Okinawa Institute of Science and Technology.

His group built these tools for a much older problem, and Lénárd Szánthó, a senior bioinformatician in the unit, adapted them to a far shorter stretch of time.

“These methods were built to look back hundreds of millions and billions of years, so it was not obvious that they would tell us anything about something this recent,” said Szöllősi.

“Lénárd made them work for this problem, and the picture that comes out is that bacteria in industrialized hosts have been acquiring genes from their neighbors recently, and this is what makes their genomes larger.”

About 5% of the gene families in a typical species collection tracked with the host’s way of life.

The team checked that against bacterial DNA read straight from the stool of 1,015 people, and 83% of the signals pointed the same way.

A few gene families turned up in more than one species, which is harder to put down to chance.

Two of them help build the outer coat of the cell, the part that other microbes and the immune system meet.

Another repeat hit was an enzyme that breaks down penicillin-type drugs. Genes like that ride the same mobile DNA now spreading antibiotic resistance through cities and farms.

The team also counted genes where protein-changing mutations had built up faster than mutations that did not change the protein. That pattern is the usual sign of a change being favored.

About 1.4% of the genes examined qualified. One of them helps a cell hold its outer envelope together under stress, and it carried the signature in three separate species.

In the five species with the most genomes, the researchers tracked single-letter DNA differences in core genes.

The variants tied to industrialized hosts kept landing on the same few jobs: building the cell wall, handling stress and making vitamin B12.

A companion study from the same group found more vitamin B12 production in the gut communities of people more exposed to industrialized environments.

Another cluster of changes hit the genes that build a molecule on the bacterial surface. The human immune system reads that molecule and reacts to it.

Researchers have not tested whether any of this changes how a person’s immune system behaves. The authors wrote that it is a possibility worth following up.

“I hope our results will inspire further studies to use this knowledge in supporting us to live a healthier life in our changing environment,” Szánthó said.

The analysis can’t say which part of industrialized living is responsible. Diet, medicine, sanitation and exposure to outdoor microbes all travel together, and this data can’t pull them apart.

Bigger genomes may not be an adaptation at all.

Extra genes build up on their own when the pressure to discard useless DNA eases, and the authors couldn’t rule that out.

Ten species is a thin slice of a gut that holds hundreds, and most of the ten belong to one bacterial group.

Species that have never been grown in a lab are missing here, which matters because industrialized guts hold fewer bacterial species to begin with.

The study itself doesn’t test what those extra genes actually do inside a person. Getting that answer will take experiments rather than more genomes.

The authors call for controlled experiments with bacteria, laboratory studies of evolution, and more detailed measurements of people’s living conditions.

The full study was published in the journal Cell.

1970 Chevy Chevelle Malibu For Sale With 8,000 Miles Shown Pairs 555 Big-Block V8 And 6-Speed Stick

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If you've got a big wad of cash burning a hole in your pocket, and you are looking for a way to celebrate being American right now, buddy, I've got the perfect car for you. For sale currently on Bring A Trailer is an absolutely gorgeous 1970 Chevy Chevelle Malibu Sport Coupe, and it's calling your name, my fellow countryman.

Of course, this is no ordinary Chevelle Malibu. While it won't appeal to the purists among us, restomod muscle car fans should certainly take note. Under the high-rise cowl hood of this beast is a Keith Black 555 cubic inch (9.1 liters for the Europeans out there) V8, and it's bolted to a Richmond six-speed manual transmission. If you want an automatic, you're barking up the wrong tree. Power is then sent to a 12-bolt Positraction rear axle, and while there's no word on what sort of power it's putting down, you can pretty safely assume that it's a lot.

Other go-fast (and slow-down) bits include Wilwood disc brakes at all four corners, Hotchkis suspension components, QA1 coilovers, an aluminum fuel cell, Hooker headers, electric exhaust cutouts and 18″ Budnik billet wheels, which certainly will not be to everyone's taste. Luckily, they'll be quite easy to swap out, and the rest of the exterior is pretty tastefully done with black paint, white rally stripes, and SS 454-style badging.

On the inside, you'll find Procar front bucket seats, a factory-style rear bench, AutoMeter gauges, and an aftermarket stereo that looks straight out of 2002 with a trunk-mounted CD changer. Continuing with bad wheel choices, the steering wheel could probably use a change as well.

While the Chevelle's original miles are unknown, according to the Bring A Trailer listing, just 8,000 miles are shown on the dashboard, and the Nevada-titled Chevy has been in the seller's family since his father bought it in 1990. All in all, it looks to be in rather good condition. The only real flaw of note mentioned in the listing is some cracking in sections of the paint finish. From more than a foot away, this baby is minty inside and out.

That 555 V8 was installed sometime between 2001 and 2005, according to the listing, and it breathes through a Holley 1050-cfm carb and a Weiand polished aluminum intake manifold. The transmission that sits behind it was installed a bit earlier, in 2000.

One of my favorite details about this car has to be the custom-made six-speed shifter, which looks mean as hell. It almost seems to be milled from a solid piece of metal, and it sticks out of a rubber shift boot mounted to the floor with "555" and "SS" written on it. Like the rest of this Chevelle Malibu, it looks the business.

If this is all speaking to you, then I suggest heading over to Bring A Trailer, because you've got until November 11 and about 1:45 p.m. to place your bid. Is 11/11 going to be your lucky day? I think so.

Trump offers UFC chief Dana White press secretary job

📅 2026-11-07  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

President Donald Trump offered UFC president and CEO Dana White the job of press secretary on Saturday.

In a post on Truth Social, Trump said: "I am pleased to announce that the Great Dana White, a violent Fighter and Warrior, has been offered the job of Press Secretary of the United States."

"We need these people to defend us against the Lunatic Left," Trump wrote. "Dana will be a Press Secretary like none other. Dana, please take the job!"

CNN, citing a source, reported White had declined the president's offer.

Earlier Saturday, White posted a picture of himself on Instagram stories standing behind the press secretary's lectern in the White House, with the caption, "On to the next chapter."

It was unclear whether White had accepted the position or was simply acknowledging the president's request as a joke.

Trump said that he was offering White the job of "Press Secretary of the United States," but the president's spokesperson is called the "White House press secretary." Trump also recently speculated about considering Trump loyalist and rapper Nicki Minaj as press secretary, in an interview with podcaster Benny Johnson.

Trump said the job offer to White extended to his assistants, "even though some are not from our Country." The so-called assistants Trump named included current and former UFC mixed martial arts fighters from countries such as Brazil and Russia.

In October, White House press secretary Katie Zacharia left the position just five days after her stint as Karoline Leavitt's replacement began.

The president told reporters he suggested Zacharia quit after her first and only press briefing, where, in her six minutes answering questions behind the lectern, she only took questions from three conservative outlets.

"I just spoke to her about it. I said, 'Katie, is this for you?'" Trump said outside the White House on Oct. 16.

The president's appeal to White follows Friday's announcement that Trump's deputy chief of staff, Stephen Miller, would be promoted to chief of staff, replacing Susie Wiles.

Trump's stunning shake-up came just three days after Democrats won a landslide victory in the midterm elections, gaining majority control in the House and Senate.

The president and White have a longstanding friendship. During Trump's first campaign in 2016, White praised the then-Republican nominee and called him a "fighter."

"Nobody took us seriously. Nobody. Except Donald Trump. Donald was the first guy to recognize the potential we saw in the UFC and encouraged us to build our business," White said.

And for his 80th birthday in June, President Trump hosted a UFC match — dubbed UFC Freedom 250 — on the White House's South Lawn.

The White House and the UFC didn't immediately respond to a request for comment.

— CNBC's Kevin Breuninger contributed to this report.

Why Opendoor Stock Jumped 13.8% Today

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Opendoor jumped as a Democratic sweep pushed oil and Treasury yields lower, but this is a rate-sensitive stock with a model I'm still skeptical of.

Opendoor Technologies (OPEN +13.79%) stock finished Wednesday, Nov. 4, 2026, up 13.8% after a Democratic sweep in the midterms set off a rally in housing stocks.

The S&P 500 (^GSPC +1.58%) and the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +1.44%) finished up 1.7% and 1.5%, respectively.

Democrats took both chambers of Congress on Tuesday night in a sweeping victory. The party has said that affordability is a major policy priority, including the cost of housing.

Beyond that, the results led oil prices lower as investors hoped for a quicker resolution to the Iran war now that Democrats control the legislature. That could help cool inflation, which in turn may mean long-term Treasury yields retreat -- exactly what the 10-year Treasury yield did today.

Opendoor is in the business of buying and selling homes, so the more active the housing market is, the better the company's sales. If yields move lower, mortgage rates follow suit, which typically means more buying and selling.

Opendoor also sits on a large, financed inventory. Higher rates eat into its bottom line as it is forced to pay more in interest.

The company reports Q3 results after the close on Thursday, with Wall Street looking for sales growth of about 17%. We'll see if they deliver.

Regardless, I'm still skeptical of the model long term. I'd be careful reading too much into today.

Investing in Your 20s – Take the Match, Open a Roth IRA, and Own Stocks

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The credit card, banking, and other financial offers that appear on this site are from companies that compensate us – for example, when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. This compensation may influence how and where offers appear on the site, including the order in which they appear on category pages. It does not influence our evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We do not include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Money you invest in your 20s has about 40 years to grow, so small amounts go far. Contribute enough to your 401(k) to get the full employer match, open a Roth IRA while your tax rate is low, and invest both accounts mostly in stocks through a target-date or broad stock index fund.

Starting early can matter more than the amount you put in, since money you invest at 25 gets 10 more years to compound than money you invest at 35.

A Roth IRA suits your 20s. You pay income tax on what you put in at today’s rate, and in retirement the money and its growth come out tax-free. That trade pays off most when your tax rate is low, and on a first salary it’s likely the lowest it will be in your career.

With about 40 years until retirement, you can hold mostly stocks, which swing more than bonds but have historically grown more. If the market drops 30% when you’re 25, every contribution buys shares for 30% less while prices stay down, and you won’t need to sell for decades. Keep buying through it.

Take the steps in this order. First, get the full match, the money your employer adds when you contribute, as soon as your plan allows. Build a starter emergency fund, a small cash cushion for surprise bills, at the same time. Next, pay off any high-interest debt, such as a credit card balance. Then, if your income is under the IRS’s ceiling for Roth contributions, open a Roth IRA and contribute up to the yearly limit, $7,500. Anything past that goes back into your 401(k).

In both accounts, pick a low-cost target-date fund named for the year you plan to retire. It holds mostly stocks while that year is far off and shifts toward bonds as it nears. A broad stock index fund, which owns small slices of hundreds or thousands of companies, works too.

At a hypothetical 7% average annual return, raising your contributions from 6% to 10% of a $50,000 salary adds $2,000 a year that grows to about $400,000 over 40 years. So each time your pay goes up, save another 1% or 2% of it until you and your employer together put in 15% of your pay.

To choose a firm for your Roth IRA, compare the best places to open an IRA.

Opinions expressed here are ours alone, not those of any advertiser. The offers that appear on this site are from companies that compensate us. That compensation may influence which products we cover and where and how they appear on a page – including the order in which they appear – but it does not influence our evaluations, ratings, or opinions. Purdue returns to Ross-Ade Stadium this Saturday, looking to take care of business against Maryland in an important Big Ten matchup. According to the latest Fan Duel Odds, the Boilermakers are approximately 5.5 -point favorites (-105). Maryland enters as a +176 moneyline underdog, while Purdue sits at -215. The opening betting line suggests that oddsmakers favor Purdue, but the Boilermakers still have plenty to prove on the field.

The over/under is currently set at 57.5 points, with both the over and under carrying -110 odds. That number suggests FanDuel is anticipating a reasonably competitive contest with offensive production on both sides.

Purdue's ability to establish its offense at home will be a major factor in determining whether this game goes over the projected total.

Maryland enters the matchup following a 20-17 loss to Illinois, while Purdue is coming off a 31-17 defeat against Penn State. Neither team is exactly riding a wave of momentum, making Saturday an important opportunity to get back in the win column.

Maryland has struggled defensively throughout the season, and Purdue will need to take advantage of that weakness.

The biggest question for Purdue is whether it can put together four complete quarters of football. The Boilermakers have had opportunities to compete in Big Ten games this season, but mistakes and inconsistent offensive execution have made winning difficult.

Playing at Ross-Ade should provide a boost, and this is a matchup Purdue should feel confident about. Maryland has struggled against stronger competition, and Purdue has a legitimate opportunity to control the game if it can establish the run and avoid costly turnovers.

Purdue has enough advantages in this matchup to come away with a much-needed victory. Maryland should be able to move the football enough to keep things interesting, but I like the Boilermakers to pull away in the second half.

I'll take Purdue to win and cover the -5.5-point spread, with the final score prediction being Purdue 34, Maryland 24. I'll also lean toward the over 57.5, as I expect both offenses to contribute to a game that finishes above the projected total.

SunCoke Energy: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Debt paydown and rising cash flow give the company room to reward shareholders while maintaining operational momentum.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 27, 2026, SunCoke Energy (SXC -2.70%) announced that it expects to modestly exceed the high end of its full-year 2026 guidance for consolidated Adjusted EBITDA, or earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization. Reliable operational performance across both operating divisions and reduced debt levels bolster long-term capital balance.

Three key takeaways from management's discussion stand out for investors.

Management reached a settlement regarding a 2025 contract dispute with steel maker Algoma Steel. This resolution eliminates working capital friction and helps boost expected 2026 free cash flow, which is cash generated after funding capital expenses, to between $180 million and $185 million.

The Domestic Coke segment, which produces high-carbon coal fuel for steel production, continues to see steady structural demand. Solid customer contracts and completed spot market sales mean the division locked in its full operational capacity for the year.

The Industrial Services handling and logistics segment experienced elevated river and terminal throughput. Higher volumes and integrated operations from the Phoenix terminal acquisition drove results above previous full-year projections.

Operations run at full capacity across core facilities, and SunCoke Energy is capturing higher terminal throughput. Long-term customer contracts provide stable revenue while management monitors debt payoff and market demand.

Capital allocation stays focused on strengthening the balance sheet and rewarding shareholders. Paying down the revolving credit line, a flexible borrowing facility, remains the primary debt target alongside ongoing dividends.

TKO reports $1.36 billion in Q3 revenue, with UFC alone pulling in $519.2 million from July to September

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

TKO Group Holdings LLC, the parent company to UFC and WWE, reported revenues of $1.36 billion in its 2026 third-quarter earnings report on Wednesday, citing the substantial financial growth of the UFC as the main driver.

According to the earnings report released by the company, UFC revenues increased by $194 million in the third quarter of 2026 — spanning July 1 through Sept. 30 — bringing total UFC revenues for the quarter to $519.2 million. It also reported third-quarter revenues of $399.3 million for WWE, which represents a decrease of $2.8 million.

"Our third quarter performance reflects the secular tailwinds powering live events and experiences around the world, and TKO's assets sit front and center of that premium ecosystem," TKO President and COO Mark Shapiro said in the release. "As we close out what we've called our year of execution, our decision to raise full-year guidance for the second time this year is the clearest expression of our conviction across the entire portfolio.

"Our business is durable and our fundamentals are solid, with the vast majority of our existing revenues locked in for the next five to seven years," Shapiro continued. "Macro trends are firmly in our favor, every facet of sports consumption is pointing north, and we see substantial runway ahead."

According to Wednesday's earnings report, the bulk of the increase in UFC revenue was due to a $132.3 million increase in the category of media rights, production and content. The report noted that this increase was "primarily related to higher media rights fees, which reflected the impact of the new distribution agreement" with Skydance, which brought UFC events to Paramount+ at the beginning of the year.

UFC revenues also benefited from "an increase in ticket sales revenue, which reflected the record-setting performance of UFC 329 and two additional events." UFC 329 saw the return of former UFC two-division champion Conor McGregor, which helped propel the event to a $26,430,566 live gate — a new UFC record.

On the WWE side, the slight dip in revenue was attributed to "a $24.8 million decrease in live events and hospitality revenue," driven in part by fewer premium live events, as well as decreased ticket sales for the company's SummerSlam event in August.

Overall, TKO again increased its target revenue for the year, raising the expected mark from $5.79 billion to $5.83 billion for the fiscal year.

Yearly total revenue for the UFC currently sits at more than $1.45 billion, per the report, while yearly total revenue for the WWE currently sits at more than $1.49 billion.

Trump Has Shifted the U.S.-Pakistan Relationship

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

South Asia Brief: Trump Has Shifted the U.S.-Pakistan Relationship

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Welcome to Foreign Policy’s South Asia Brief.

The highlights this week: A proposed liquefied natural gas project in Pakistan underscores close ties with the Trump administration, garment workers in Bangladesh protest for pay increases, and India’s opposition alliance rallies to call for the resignation of the chief election commissioner.

Welcome to Foreign Policy’s South Asia Brief.

The highlights this week: A proposed liquefied natural gas project in Pakistan underscores close ties with the Trump administration, garment workers in Bangladesh protest for pay increases, and India’s opposition alliance rallies to call for the resignation of the chief election commissioner.

Since U.S. President Donald Trump returned to the White House last year, Pakistan has banked heavily on doing business with the United States. With the Democrats flipping both houses of Congress in the U.S. midterm elections on Tuesday, though, one has to wonder if Islamabad could come out as another loser.

For one thing, scrutiny of Trump’s business dealings will certainly increase, constraining his administration’s capacity to pursue deals abroad.

A big topic of discussion in Pakistan this week—along with the midterms—is the news that a consortium of U.S. companies has proposed a $4.4 billion liquefied natural gas (LNG) project in Port Qasim, near Karachi. It would be Pakistan’s first LNG storage facility, helping to shield the country from global energy shocks.

The proposal entails a large-scale storage terminal and eventually a 680-mile delivery pipeline. The plan is backed by the U.S. Trade and Development Agency, U.S. International Development Finance Corp., and the Export-Import Bank of the United States.

Washington’s support for the proposed LNG project underscores just how much the U.S.-Pakistan relationship has changed in Trump’s second term. Bilateral ties were downgraded after the U.S. withdrawal from Afghanistan in 2021, in large part because Washington had long viewed relations with Islamabad through a security lens.

The Trump administration turned this approach on its head. The United States now prioritizes ties with Pakistan, and Trump often praises the country’s leadership. Though counterterrorism still figures in the relationship, Washington has emphasized economic and commercial issues in its engagement with Islamabad.

In particular, the United States has taken a strong interest in Pakistan’s critical minerals and cryptocurrency resources. It has pushed for the country to import more U.S. goods, including energy supplies. In October 2025, Pakistan received its first-ever shipment of U.S. crude oil. Washington has pushed to remove trade obstacles with Islamabad, including ending a two-year ban on U.S. soybean exports.

Pakistan, which had long pitched its economic offerings to the United States unsuccessfully, has capitalized on the Trump administration’s newfound interest in its assets. The Financial Times reported last year that Islamabad went as far as to offer Washington the opportunity to build and manage a port on the Arabian Sea. (Pakistan publicly denies this.)

The proposed LNG project would be one of the Trump administration’s most ambitious commercial ventures in Pakistan, perhaps putting an exclamation point on the shift in the U.S. approach to the country. Yet that shift might not even be sustainable—especially given the midterm results.

The Trump administration’s commercial focus is largely driven by the president’s whims and by the economic interests of his family members and close associates. But this arrangement won’t outlast Trump’s term, which ends in two years. Further, with Congress in the hands of the Democrats starting in January, it could also become more difficult to pull off.

Conversations with Pakistani interlocutors this week revealed a combination of curiosity and concern about ties with the United States in the Trump era. There seems to be a desire to understand how the relationship improved so quickly alongside a sense that it might not last for long, given Trump’s unpredictability. The Democrats’ victory will only sharpen these concerns.

These worries reflect an evergreen challenge for the U.S.-Pakistan relationship: how to craft a strong partnership that can withstand political change in both countries and weather external shocks.

The last time the White House officially announced a policy on Pakistan was during Trump’s first term, in August 2017, when a new South Asia strategy included a hard-line approach toward Islamabad. It’s clear that the United States is overdue in conducting a formal review of its Pakistan policy, even as the relationship is being informally reevaluated and refashioned.

Garment protests in Bangladesh. Garment workers have taken to the streets in industrial areas of Dhaka, Bangladesh’s capital, in recent days to call for pay increases. Clashes with security forces have prompted the closure of dozens of garment factories and raised concerns about shipment delays.

Garment workers are a critical constituency in Bangladesh, numbering around 4 million, and the sector accounts for 80 percent of the country’s export earnings. Bangladesh’s ready-made garment industry is a global success story; it made the country one of South Asia’s best-performing economies until a slump in the last few years.

With the downturn, Bangladesh seems to be paying the price for relying so heavily on a single export—as globally competitive as it was—for so long. The protests are just the latest policy challenge for Dhaka, which is already struggling with economic stress, energy shortages, and the world’s most serious measles crisis.

These issues risk distracting Bangladesh from pursuing economic and governance reforms—something the government, which took office in February, was given a mandate to prioritize.

India’s opposition alliance rallies. On Sunday, the main opposition alliance in India staged protests in New Delhi to call for the resignation of India’s chief election commissioner, Gyanesh Kumar. The rally drew tens of thousands of people and marked the latest mobilization against a voter roll revision that critics say favored the ruling Bharatiya Janata Party.

The youth-led Cockroach Janta Party has spearheaded the protests calling for Kumar to step down. This summer, the Cockroach movement galvanized protests over leaked exam papers that ultimately resulted in the resignation of the country’s education minister.

Now, India’s opposition is riding on the coattails of this activism. The success of the Cockroach movement is a gift to the opposition, which has long been hobbled by the absence of a popular leader and disagreements within its alliance.

Heat stress becomes serious vulnerability. The International Labor Organization (ILO) released studies last week warning that by 2040, workplace heat stress spawned by rising temperatures could lead to reduced working time equivalent to around 145,000 full-time jobs in Sri Lanka and 2,400 in the Maldives.

The reports estimate that these losses will be valued at around $26 billion in Sri Lanka and $576 million in the Maldives. The hardest-hit sector in Sri Lanka will be agriculture, along with services, manufacturing, and construction. In the Maldives, services and construction will be the most vulnerable industries.

The ILO findings are a reminder of the high economic cost of climate change in a region where economies are fragile—and where some of the most labor-dependent sectors are especially vulnerable to climate effects.

Last Sunday, around 200,000 people converged on the Bhutanese town of Gelephu to watch volunteers construct 108 stupas. (The number 108 is sacred in Buddhism.) The initiative is part of a campaign led by Bhutan’s king to transform Gelephu, near Bhutan’s border with India, into a “mindfulness city.”

Bhutan hopes to use the initiative, which emphasizes ecological sustainability and happiness, as an antidote to the country’s deepening economic stress. The stupa project is certainly an unorthodox way to tackle these problems. Critics will likely say it does little to reverse rising unemployment and a rapid outflow of highly skilled workers.

However, the project aligns with a long-standing Bhutanese principle known as “gross national happiness,” which measures national well-being not by income or other economic indicators but instead by the level of contentment among citizens.

Michael Kugelman is the writer of Foreign Policy’s weekly South Asia Brief and a senior fellow for South Asia at the Atlantic Council. An analyst of the region for nearly two decades, he previously directed the South Asia Institute at the Wilson Center. X: @michaelkugelman

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: Medigap Plan G: UHC vs. Healthspring

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Do you think a HD-G maybe harder to manage deductible payments if you are in your eighties or nineties?
Much much harder. Plan G is the way to go. You meet you deductible which requires somewhere between one and four payments and then you're done for the year. Even that is annoying. But I cannot imagine having to deal with a high deductible plan at age 95 or even 85.
Of course we don't know where the deductible for the HD plans will be in 10 years, but it is very hard to reach the deductible even with some serious medical conditions. My wife had a couple serious things at once and we didn't satisfy the deductible until November.

My point is that at the current reimbursement levels, it is hard to even satisfy the deductible. Now, there is some charm is not have to deal with any bills of any type and of course, we can't predict the future w/r/t deductibles.

Why Wayfair Stock Is Up Today

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Shares of Wayfair (W +10.99%) leaped 11% on Wednesday after the home goods-focused e-commerce leader delivered stronger-than-expected profits despite a tepid overall economic environment.

Wayfair's net revenue rose 9.7% year over year to $3.4 billion in the third quarter.

The gains were fueled by an 11.4% jump in U.S. net revenue to $3 billion, marking the online shopping platform's fastest pace of growth since the early stages of the pandemic.

"At the start of this year, we told you we expected to take share in the U.S. at a pace we had not seen in several years," CEO Niraj Shah said. "With U.S. revenue growing double digits against a roughly flat category here in Q3, we've done exactly that."

Wayfair's active customers grew 4.7% to 22.2 million as of Sept. 30.

Although average order value declined to $311 from $317 the prior-year quarter, customers are ordering more often and spending more overall.

Orders delivered in the third quarter jumped 12.2% to 11 million, while last 12-month net revenue per active customer increased 3.1% to $596.

All told, Wayfair's adjusted earnings per share surged 33% to $0.93. That was well above Wall Street's estimates, which had called for per-share profits of $0.79.

Looking ahead, Wayfair sees "high single-digit growth" in revenue in the fourth quarter.

"The momentum we have on orders and customers gives us confidence that our share gains are structural, and we are still in the early innings," Shah said.

Joe Tenebruso has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool recommends Wayfair. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

*Average returns of all recommendations since inception. Cost basis and return based on previous market day close.

AMC Theatres Sets All-Time Record for Quarterly Revenue, Thanks to ‘Spider-Man: Brand New Day’ and ‘The Odyssey’

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The blockbuster success of “Spider-Man: Brand New Day” and “The Odyssey” fueled a record-breaking quarter for AMC Theatres. Total revenue for the three-month period ended Sept. 30 hit $1.71 billion, up 31.6% from $1.3 billion a year earlier.

Those marked the largest returns ever posted by the world’s largest cinema chain, eclipsing the previous all-time high established in the second quarter of 2026, led by “Project Hail Mary and “Michael.” Prior to this year’s rebound, AMC’s quarterly revenue high-water mark was pro-COVID, set in the second quarter of 2019, when “Avengers: Endgame” was released and shattered any and all box office expectations. Related Stories 'Magic Atlas' Review: Surreal, Shapeshifting Animation Is a Sometimes Mystifying Technical Marvel 'Capturing Bigfoot' Review: An Astounding Documentary Uncovers the Truth

“In recent years, where other key players in our industry collapsed, at AMC we repeatedly instead have talked about recovery. Today, we can finally say that AMC has recovered,” AMC CEO Adam Aron said in a statement. “To our studio partners, lenders, business partners, employees and our millions of passionate shareholders, thank you for standing with us as we traveled through a long, and at times brutal, road. Mere survival was never the goal. Our goal ultimately was to thrive, and that is exactly and precisely where our focus now lies. We are highly energized by the tremendous opportunities ahead.”

Aron attributed the better-than-expected quarter to several factors, including the rising popularity of premium large formats, the A-List subscription service and a resurgent box office. This summer was the best since COVID, as “Spider-Man: Brand New Day” became the third-highest grossing movie in history with $2.5 billion and Nolan’s ‘The Odyssey” powered to an astounding $1.8 billion. Overall domestic theatrical revenue has already eclipsed $8 billion, a milestone the industry didn’t cross last year until December. With potential juggernauts like “Avengers: Doomsday,” “Dune: Part III” and “The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping” still to come on the schedule, this year will easily be the biggest at the box office in post-pandemic times.

Now when people are going to the movies, they’ve been more likely to indulge in popcorn and soda. AMC’s food and beverage revenue hit $589.5 million for the three-month period, up 30% from $451.8 million a year earlier. Meanwhile, ticket revenue rose to $954.5 million, a 33% increase from the $715.1 million a year earlier.

Losses also shrank for AMC. The company’s net loss was $109.3 million, compared with $298.2 million in losses in the third quarter of 2025. It logged a diluted loss per share of 12 cents, after reporting a loss of 58 cents in the prior-year quarter.

“Sometimes short but sweet is the best way to summarize performance,” Aron wrote in a five-paragraph statement. “We expect the remaining film slate to propel both the domestic and global box office to their highest full-year levels in seven years. We could not be more enthusiastic about AMC’s prospects as this year draws to a triumphant close.”

Durbin Is Retiring, Marshall Just Lost—Why The Bill Threatening Your Credit Card Rewards Isn’t Dead

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Dick Durbin is retiring and Roger Marshall just lost his Senate seat. The two most prominent champions of the Credit Card Competition Act, the law intended to regulate credit card interchange which will mean far fewer rewards for consumers, will be gone in January. The bill threatening the economics of card rewards, and the airlines that depend on them, has support from President Trump and Vice President JD Vance was an original co-sponsor so it may come back with his presidential campaign. But the thing about this legislation is that it’s more useful as a way to raise campaign money from both retailers and banks than as something Congress actually passes. Departures of Durbin and Marshall haven’t made the Durbin-Marshall bill go away. Durbin identified Vermont Senator Peter Welch as his successor on the issue. The Airline Lobby Is Right To Worry In this week’s Airlines Confidential, Airlines for America CEO Chris Sununu warns that state initiatives interfering with card fees would eliminate points-funded travel. Banks buy miles because rewards help attract customers and their spending. By reducing margins on that spending, it’s less valuable to spend to encourage consumers to use the product. In markets where government has gotten involved in interchange rates we’ve seen: smaller initial bonuses higher annual fees annual caps on awards award chart devaluations (if each mile is worth less, an airline can still award one mile per dollar on spend despite receiving less money for it from the bank) It also means less revenue for the airline. In the third quarter, American Express represented all of Delta’s profit. American Airlines is barely breaking even even with several billion net from Citibank. We’d have much shakier airlines and fewer flights, and aspirational leisure destinations like Hawaii in particular would see less air service. What The Credit Card Competition Act Actually Does The current Senate bill, S. 3623, would require covered issuers with more than $100 billion in assets to enable at least two unaffiliated payment networks. At least one would have to be outside of Visa and Mastercard. Merchants get to choose the routing. That’s meant to put downward pressure on merchant payment costs by changing who controls which network handles the transaction. Cards issued in a three-party system, such as American Express issuing its own cards, are exempt from the routing requirement. Merchants benefit from accepting cards. They get more sales, and higher average sales. It’s often less expensive to accept credit cards than to take cash (due to employee theft, incorrect change, higher insurance costs). Retailers want the service cards offer, but want the government to force Visa and Mastercard to provide it for less. Trump’s promise of $1,200 in annual family savings was completely made up and inconsistent with the bill. Currently a majority of interchange gets rebated to the customer. Regulation of interchange has never lowered prices to consumers. And card payments, funded in large part by interchange, provide services to consumers beyond just payments (and borrowings). They often fund consumer protections – not just stronger dispute resolution than debit cards, but also extended warranty, return protection, and travel protections. The Bill’s Strongest Value Is As A Threat Back in July 2023, I argued that this was an especially useful issue for opening contribution wallets on both sides. Retailers have a large, concentrated interest in lowering their acceptance costs. Banks, payment networks and airlines have a large, concentrated interest in preserving the existing economics. An unresolved legislative threat gives both sides a reason to pour dollars into campaigns and political committees. I’d still bet on another round of proposals, threatened amendments and lobbying. Durbin and Marshall are leaving but the retailers and banks with billions at stake aren’t. Politicians still want the money. Banks, payment networks and airlines have a large, concentrated interest in preserving the existing economics. An unresolved legislative threat gives both sides a reason to pour dollars into campaigns and political committees.

I’d still bet on another round of proposals, threatened amendments and lobbying. Durbin and Marshall are leaving but the retailers and banks with billions at stake aren’t. Politicians still want the money.

Year-end giving campaign launches for the AAN Research Program

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Support the urgent need for brain research funding

MINNEAPOLIS, Nov. 4, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- The need for brain research funding has never been more urgent. That is why the American Academy of Neurology (AAN) today announced its year-end giving campaign for the AAN Research Program, inviting people to support the researchers working to find new treatments and cures for brain diseases and disorders.

One hundred percent of every tax-deductible donation will go directly to brain research.

"Scientific breakthroughs begin with researchers who have the ideas and expertise to pursue important questions," said American Academy of Neurology President Natalia S. Rost, MD, MPH, FAAN, FAHA. "The AAN Research Program gives all of us the opportunity to help advance high-quality brain research, bringing hope to people affected by brain disease."

Brain research is what makes it possible to prevent, diagnose, treat, and eventually even cure conditions of the brain and nervous system. It also advances our understanding of memory, learning, sleep, mental health, and healthy aging, helping people live healthier lives.

The AAN Research Program has funded brain research for more than 30 years, owing to its success the exceptional pool of talented researchers in the AAN community, the world's largest association of neurologists and neuroscience professionals.

Demand for funding, however, continues to outpace available resources. Hundreds of researchers apply for AAN grants. The AAN invested nearly $1.8 million in 2026, but millions in viable neuroscience research went unfunded.

"We know that excellent research proposals go unfunded," said American Academy of Neurology Chief Executive Officer Mary E. Post, MBA, CAE. "We want to make sure that we have the funding to support the research that may eventually lead to a cure for MS or a way to prevent Alzheimer's. Every contribution can help researchers pursue discoveries as they work toward improving brain health for all."

With neurologists leading the AAN Board of Directors, Science Committee, and Research Program Subcommittee, the AAN is guided by trusted experts who understand both the science and the real-world application of research.

Help advance research by making a tax-deductible gift on the BrainHealth.com "Donate to Research" page. Organizations interested in supporting the AAN Research Program may contact donations@aan.com.

The AAN Research Program is administered through the American Academy of Neurology Institute, the Academy's 501(c)(3) charitable organization.

How Much Premium Economy Costs On London-New York Flights In 2026

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Premium economy on a London-New York flight can cost more than twice as much as an economy ticket, but the difference between the two cabins and business class can sometimes be surprisingly small. For passengers facing an approximately eight-hour transatlantic journey, a wider seat, extra legroom, better meals, and extra baggage can be appealing, but does the experience justify the added cost?

In 2026, passengers traveling between London Heathrow Airport (LHR) and New York JFK Airport have several airlines to choose from, including British Airways, Virgin Atlantic, American Airlines, Delta Air Lines, and JetBlue. Those flying to Newark Liberty International Airport (EWR) have fewer nonstop options, primarily British Airways and United Airlines. With different premium economy products, competing fares, and varying baggage allowances, how much should passengers actually expect to pay for a more comfortable transatlantic journey?

Premium economy on the London-New York route typically costs approximately $1,000 to $1,300 for a return journey, compared with around $500 to $600 for a standard economy ticket. This means passengers can expect to pay roughly twice as much for the upgraded cabin, although the difference depends on the airline, travel dates, departure airport, and availability.

According to our analysis of premium economy fares in 2026, the transatlantic market demonstrates how airlines position their intermediate cabin between standard economy and business class. Premium economy has become a major revenue opportunity for carriers, letting them charge more for added comfort without offering the considerably more expensive lie-flat seating associated with business class.

However, the difference between cabins isn't always consistent. A NerdWallet comparison of British Airways fares found that one evening departure from New York JFK to London offered premium economy for $1,162, more than twice the $532 economy fare. Business class was available for just $1,222, meaning passengers would have saved only $60 by selecting premium economy instead. Another comparison showed a return premium economy fare of $1,459 against $737 in economy. These examples raise an important question: how much competition exists between airlines on the route, and where can passengers find the best selection?

The greatest selection of nonstop services is available between London Heathrow and New York JFK. According to FlightConnections, five airlines serve the route: British Airways, Virgin Atlantic, American Airlines, Delta Air Lines, and JetBlue. The route covers approximately 3,440 miles (5,536 kilometers), with flights generally taking around eight hours.

However, passengers should distinguish between the airlines operating the route and those offering a dedicated premium economy cabin. British Airways calls its intermediate product World Traveller Plus, Virgin Atlantic markets its cabin as Premium, and American Airlines offers Premium Economy on eligible long-haul aircraft. Delta also operates Premium Select on eligible international services. JetBlue, meanwhile, offers its Mint business class and Even More Space economy seating rather than a conventional dedicated premium economy cabin.

The situation is different for passengers traveling to Newark. According to FlightConnections' LHR-EWR route information, British Airways and United Airlines operate nonstop flights between the two airports: United's intermediate product corresponding to British Airways’ World Traveller Plus is called Premium Plus. The route covers approximately 3,454 miles (5,559 km), with an average flight time of seven hours and 55 minutes.

British Airways, Virgin Atlantic, American, Delta, JetBlue

World Traveller Plus, Premium, Premium Economy, Premium Select

Such a variety of airlines at JFK makes it attractive to travelers seeking schedule flexibility and competitive fares. Nevertheless, more airline options do not automatically mean cheaper premium economy tickets. British Airways and Virgin Atlantic offer useful examples of what passengers can expect to pay.

A closer look at the price bump that is required to fly in BA's Club cabins.

British Airways and Virgin Atlantic are two of the most prominent competitors on the London-New York market, and both offer dedicated premium economy cabins on their long-haul services. Their current fares demonstrate that passengers departing London can sometimes find considerably more attractive prices than those booking an equivalent journey from the United States.

Virgin Atlantic's official website provides a particularly useful snapshot of its pricing. For a return journey from London Heathrow to New York JFK between November 17 and November 22, 2026, the airline advertised Premium fares starting at approximately $1,529. Its published offers for selected dates in May, July, and August 2027 also showed starting fares of approximately $1,542 to $1,549. These are advertised starting prices, not guaranteed fares for every departure.

The airline's official Premium flight deals page also provides information about its cabin and included services. British Airways, meanwhile, allows passengers to compare World Traveller Plus fares through its official website.

Furthermore, the KAYAK sample for February 5–12, 2027 provides another useful comparison point during low season, with prices slightly lower than those for immediate departures within two weeks, although those dates fall outside the 2026 calendar year. With the fares set, the next question is what passengers get for spending more than £1,000 on a return ticket.

One of the main advantages of premium economy is extra personal space, which can make a big difference on an overnight transatlantic journey or for passengers with health issues. British Airways' World Traveller Plus cabin features wider seats, additional legroom, greater recline, lumbar support, and adjustable headrests and footrests. Passengers also receive an enhanced meal service, complimentary bar service, personal entertainment systems, and priority boarding.

Virgin Atlantic's Premium cabin offers a similarly upgraded experience, although the precise seat specifications depend on the aircraft operating the flight. According to its official cabin information, the airline offers up to 38 inches of seat pitch, additional room to stretch out, and a footrest. Its onboard experience also includes restaurant-inspired meals served on porcelain dishware, a welcome glass of bubbly, and additional snacks on selected aircraft.

Baggage is another important consideration when calculating the actual cost difference between economy and premium economy. Virgin Atlantic Premium includes two checked bags weighing up to 50 pounds (23 kg) each, plus a 22-pound (10-kg) hand luggage allowance. British Airways World Traveller Plus also provides two checked bags on most routes, while some economy fares are hand-baggage-only tickets. According to NerdWallet, adding two checked bags to certain British Airways economy bookings can cost up to $175 each way.

These additional services make premium economy more than simply an upgraded seat. However, the cabin doesn't offer the same experience as business class, especially for sleeping arrangements and airport lounge access. This distinction becomes important when the price difference between the two cabins starts to narrow.

The price difference between premium economy and business class fares is not always huge. Airlines use dynamic pricing systems that adjust ticket prices according to demand, remaining inventory, booking patterns, and the number of seats available in each fare category. As a result, a premium economy ticket can occasionally approach the price of a discounted business class seat, especially when demand for the intermediate cabin is high.

NerdWallet’s British Airways example illustrates this well. One New York JFK departure offered premium economy for $1,162, compared with $532 for economy and $1,222 for business class. In this comparison, choosing premium economy instead of business class saved only $60. For passengers seeking the additional comfort of a fully flat bed on an overnight flight, that relatively small difference could make business class a more attractive option.

Passengers also have another option when considering British Airways: using Avios. According to Award Travel Finder's London-New York redemption analysis, a one-way off-peak World Traveller Plus redemption requires 46,750 Avios, increasing to 66,000 during peak periods. Economy requires 27,500 Avios off-peak and 33,000 during peak periods. Taxes and charges are additional, meaning passengers must compare the monetary cost of an award ticket with the cash fare before deciding whether redemption represents good value.

Available loyalty points and occasional business-class promotions make comparing all three cabins especially important. Nevertheless, for passengers without enough points or who can't find attractive business class fares, premium economy remains an interesting middle ground. The question is whether the added comfort justifies the higher cost on this transatlantic route.

Premium economy occupies an interesting niche in the London-New York market because it provides a noticeable improvement over standard economy, often without the substantial expense of business class. On an approximately eight-hour journey, additional legroom, wider seats, improved dining, and priority airport services can make a meaningful difference for some passengers.

However, the financial case weakens when the fare approaches business-class pricing. A passenger paying $1,162 for premium economy when business class costs $1,222 is arguably better off considering the higher cabin, provided the business class fare is genuinely available for the same itinerary and booking conditions. Conversely, Virgin Atlantic's advertised return fare of $1,529 for November 2026 demonstrates that premium economy can be considerably more accessible than some of the higher-priced examples found in the US market.

Competition also gives passengers some flexibility. Travelers to JFK can compare British Airways, Virgin Atlantic, American Airlines, Delta, and JetBlue, while Newark offers British Airways and United as nonstop options. Checking both New York airports, comparing departure dates, and examining the differences between economy, premium economy, and business class can reveal opportunities that aren't immediately apparent when searching for a single airline.

Found an error? Send it info@simpleflying.com so it can be corrected.

Premium economy isn't automatically worth twice the price of economy, but the cheapest available ticket isn't necessarily the best option. The deciding factor will be the actual fare difference on the selected dates, the baggage and onboard benefits included, and how much passengers value additional comfort during their transatlantic journey.

Pharming: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A rare-disease player with a stabilized core product is now targeting a market 26 times larger, fueled by cash generation that needs no external financing.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 27, 2026, Pharming (PHAR -3.64%) announced total revenue grew 21% to $117.6 million. Operating profit increased 89% to $29.9 million as the business expanded sales of its primary rare disease treatments. Strong cash generation gives the business funding to expand its clinical drug pipeline without needing extra financing.

RUCONEST, a treatment for hereditary angioedema (HAE, a rare genetic condition causing severe swelling), returned to revenue growth after earlier quarterly declines. Management maintained over 90% of active patients despite new oral competition, stabilizing demand among high-volume users.

Joenja, a therapy for activated phosphoinositide 3-kinase delta syndrome (APDS, a rare primary immunodeficiency causing immune system dysfunction), expanded through new international launches and U.S. Food and Drug Administration (FDA) pediatric approvals. International sales now generate roughly one-third of total Joenja revenue.

Management's advancing leniolisib into common variable immune deficiency (CVID, a primary immunodeficiency disorder affecting immune function), a market 26 times larger than APDS in patient prevalence. Positive phase 2 data in related primary immunodeficiencies (PIDs) provides proof of concept for the broader indication.

Operating profit is surging as commercial sales expand, and management isn't hiding its satisfaction. High patient retention and specialized manufacturing create durable competitive moats. Management is strategically watching channel inventory timing and pediatric adoption.

Disciplined execution and clinical expansion set up long-term growth across broader immunology markets.

Prediction markets cement their status after forecasting Democratic sweep of Congress

📅 2026-11-04  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Prediction market platforms cemented their role in election forecasting on Tuesday, when markets accurately predicted a host of races before the polls closed, and projected outcomes before news outlets.

Democrats were seen as favored to flip both the U.S. House of Representatives and Senate on prediction markets before the first polls closed at 6:00 p.m. ET Tuesday, and were seen as narrow favorites to take at least 53 Senate seats, while a 54th was forecast as a toss-up.

So far, Democrats have won 54 Senate seats — including wins in Iowa, which prediction markets placed at 50-50 odds, and South Carolina, where Republicans narrowly favored. Outstanding results remain in Mississippi and Alaska, where prediction markets give Democrat Scott Colom and Mary Peltola 90% odds of winning.

Leading prediction market platforms were actively projecting races on X Tuesday night. Those projections, at times, came before many media outlets made their own projections, or before even a substantial number of votes had been counted.

Kalshi was confident making those calls, as long as odds for a candidate were above 99% and trading activity for the opposite candidate "dried out,"  said Jack Such, a spokesperson for the platform.

Polymarket celebrated on X how it projected races, saying that in 10 key races, the platform called races ahead of outlets including CNN, Fox News and the Associated Press by a median 31 minutes.

"It's now routinely hours between when prediction markets call it versus the TV networks," said Polymarket CEO Shayne Coplan in an X post. "No knock to them, but now there's Polymarket, and many people are realizing they want to know what's happening in real time."

Even in races where markets initially favored the candidate who eventually lost, they quickly adjusted once initial vote counts came in. In South Carolina, Democratic Senate candidate Annie Andrews was seen as the favorite on Kalshi to win shortly after 7:30 p.m., only 30 minutes after polls closed. In Texas, where Democratic state Rep. Gina Hinojosa ousted Republican Gov. Greg Abbott, Hinojosa's odds on Polymarket jumped from 39% to 73% within five minutes of initial poll closings.

"You now have financially motivated number crunchers doing this, around the world, that give you that signal just that much faster," said Flip Pidot, a member of the board of directors at the Coalition for Political Forecasting.

Still, a successful night for prediction markets shouldn't stop efforts aimed at stronger regulation of the platforms, said Michelle Kendler-Kretsch, a researcher at the Anti-Corruption Data Collective, a nonprofit. She cited a recent Reuters/Ipsos survey that found most Americans are against using prediction markets to trade on elections.

"Risks and vulnerabilities didn't change overnight just because the markets were accurate this time around," she said.

Right before the midterms, prediction market forecasts had missed major global elections, including Brazil's presidential race, where Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva only had about 20% odds to win on Kalshi before polls closed.

Prediction markets also whiffed in some high-profile Democratic primaries over the summer, forecasting extremely high odds for progressives in Wisconsin and Michigan, for example. The Michigan Democrat, Abdul El-Sayed, narrowly won his primary before going on to win Thursday's general election.

Academics who've researched prediction markets for decades say the platforms aren't wrong when candidates with low odds on markets end up winning races.

"If you toss two coins, there's a 25% chance that you'll throw two heads in a row, and nobody would be terribly surprised if you did," said Rajiv Sethi, an economics professor at Barnard College.

Despite their early success on Tuesday, Dartmouth College economic professor Eric Zitzewitz said it's still not clear if casual observers understand how market probabilities work.

"Some people who are coming to [prediction markets] for the first time will see 55 by 45 odds and think that, 'Oh the candidate with 55 is way ahead,'" he said.

As prediction markets gain traction, however, the public will improve at understanding how to interpret their odds, Zitzewitz said.

With another election under their belts, prediction markets will remain a force through the next presidential election, experts said.

"Last time around [in 2024], prediction markets were in the discussion, but they were in the discussion in a way that was a little bit unsure," said Harry Crane, a professor of statistics at Rutgers University. "This [was] the first true prediction markets election."

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.
